Throughout this paper, Greek indices will run from 0 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-1$$\end{document}n-1 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ,\nu =0,1,2,\ldots ,n-1$$\end{document}μ,ν=0,1,2,…,n-1) and Latin indices will run from 1 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-1$$\end{document}n-1 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,b=1,2,\ldots ,n-1$$\end{document}a,b=1,2,…,n-1), unless specified otherwise. We will use M to denote a connected n-dimensional manifold and TM to denote the tangent bundle over M. All manifolds are assumed to be smooth. Points in M will generally be denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p,q\in M$$\end{document}p,q∈M. Vectors on M, which are points in TM (and consequently the conic bundle U), will be denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U. The space of vector fields over an arbitrary smooth manifold N of dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell$$\end{document}ℓ is denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma TN$$\end{document}ΓTN and its elements are denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X,Y\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}X,Y∈ΓTN. Maps between abstract manifolds are denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta :N\rightarrow P$$\end{document}Θ:N→P. The space of scalar fields over N is denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma \Lambda ^0N$$\end{document}ΓΛ0N, and we denote its elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,g,h,F,G,H\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0N$$\end{document}f,g,h,F,G,H∈ΓΛ0N depending on context. The space of r-forms is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma \Lambda ^r N$$\end{document}ΓΛrN, and we denote its elements by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta \in \Gamma \Lambda ^r N$$\end{document}α,β∈ΓΛrN. Although we consider both vectors at points and vector fields, we only consider r-form fields. Hence, when referring to r-forms, the word field is implicit. A field evaluated at a point will be denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\vert _{p}$$\end{document}X|p and the action of a vector field on a scalar field by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\langle h\rangle$$\end{document}X⟨h⟩. A table of generic and specific symbols is given in Table 1.

The internal contraction of an r-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}α by a vector field X is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_X\alpha$$\end{document}iXα (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha {:} X$$\end{document}α:X for the special case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}α is a 1-form), and the Lie derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}α along X is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_X\alpha$$\end{document}LXα. Contraction by coordinate partial derivatives are denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_\mu ^{(x)}=i_{\partial _\mu ^{(x)}}$$\end{document}iμ(x)=i∂μ(x) etc. A similar convention is used for Lie derivatives along a coordinate vector field where we denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\partial _\mu ^{(x)}}=L_\mu ^{(x)}$$\end{document}L∂μ(x)=Lμ(x) etc.

Definition 1.1

(Pullback and Pushforward) Given a map between manifolds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta :N\rightarrow P$$\end{document}Θ:N→P , then the pushforward of a vector at a point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in T_p N$$\end{document}v_∈TpN is given by3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \Theta _*\underline{v} \right) \langle h \rangle = \underline{v}\langle h\circ \Theta \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θ∗v_⟨h⟩=v_⟨h∘Θ⟩.The pullback of a scalar field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0P$$\end{document}h∈ΓΛ0P is given by4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta ^*h= (h\circ \Theta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θ∗h=(h∘Θ).We may also pullback forms of arbitrary degree r according to the following rules:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta ^*(d h)= d (\Theta ^*h) \quad \text {and}\quad \Theta ^*(\alpha \wedge \beta )=\Theta ^*\alpha \wedge \Theta ^*\beta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θ∗(dh)=d(Θ∗h)andΘ∗(α∧β)=Θ∗α∧Θ∗β,for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0N,\; \alpha \in \Gamma \Lambda ^1 N,\; \beta \in \Gamma \Lambda ^{r-1}N$$\end{document}h∈ΓΛ0N,α∈ΓΛ1N,β∈ΓΛr-1N. The pullback and pushforward satisfy the compatibility property:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha :\Theta _*\underline{v} = \Theta ^*\alpha :\underline{v}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α:Θ∗v_=Θ∗α:v_,for any 1-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \Gamma \Lambda ^1P$$\end{document}α∈ΓΛ1P and point vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in T_pN$$\end{document}v_∈TpN. Observe that we pushforward vectors at points, while we pull back r-forms.

Definition 1.2

(Tangential Vector Fields) Given an embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta :K\hookrightarrow N$$\end{document}Θ:K↪N, a vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}X∈ΓTN is tangent to the submanifold K if, there exists a vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \Gamma TK$$\end{document}Y∈ΓTK such that7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta _*(Y\vert _{p})= X\vert _{\Theta (p)}, \; \forall p\in K. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θ∗(Y|p)=X|Θ(p),∀p∈K.Note that if Y exists then it is unique. In this case, we say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \Gamma TK$$\end{document}Y∈ΓTK is induced by X. Given an r-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \Gamma \Lambda ^r N$$\end{document}α∈ΓΛrN then8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta ^*(i_X \alpha ) = i_{Y} (\Theta ^*\alpha ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θ∗(iXα)=iY(Θ∗α).

Definition 1.3

(Scalar Lift) Given a scalar field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0N$$\end{document}h∈ΓΛ0N, the scalar lift defines a scalar field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{h}\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0TN$$\end{document}h˙∈ΓΛ0TN given by9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{h}\vert _{\underline{u}}= \underline{u} \langle h \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h˙|u_=u_⟨h⟩.

Definition 1.4

(Induced Coordinates) Given a coordinate system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^0,\ldots ,x^{n-1})$$\end{document}(x0,…,xn-1) for the patch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K\subset N$$\end{document}K⊂N, this induces a coordinate system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\bar{x}^0,\ldots ,\bar{x}^{n-1},\dot{x}^0,\ldots ,\dot{x}^{n-1})$$\end{document}(x¯0,…,x¯n-1,x˙0,…,x˙n-1) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi ^{-1}(K)\subset TN$$\end{document}π-1(K)⊂TN, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{x}^\mu =\pi ^*x^\mu$$\end{document}x¯μ=π∗xμ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{x}^\mu$$\end{document}x˙μ is the scalar lift. In this coordinate system an arbitrary vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}X∈ΓTN can be written10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} X= \bar{X}^\mu \frac{\partial \;}{\partial \bar{x}^\mu }+ \hat{X}^\mu \frac{\partial \;}{\partial \dot{x}^\mu } \quad \text {where} \quad \bar{X}^\mu = X\langle {\bar{x}^\mu }\rangle \text { and } \hat{X}^\mu = X\langle {\dot{x}^\mu }\rangle . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X=X¯μ∂∂x¯μ+X^μ∂∂x˙μwhereX¯μ=X⟨x¯μ⟩andX^μ=X⟨x˙μ⟩.

Every vector field over TN can be defined using Eq. (10) and consequently can be defined entirely by its action on scalar fields of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi ^*h$$\end{document}π∗h and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{h}$$\end{document}h˙ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0N$$\end{document}h∈ΓΛ0N.

Throughout, we will use the shorthand11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _\mu ^{(x)}= \frac{\partial \;}{\partial x^\mu },\quad \partial _\mu ^{(\bar{x})}= \frac{\partial \;}{\partial \bar{x}^\mu },\quad \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}= \frac{\partial \;}{\partial \dot{x}^\mu }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂μ(x)=∂∂xμ,∂μ(x¯)=∂∂x¯μ,∂μ(x˙)=∂∂x˙μ.Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu ^{(x)}\in \Gamma TM$$\end{document}∂μ(x)∈ΓTM while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu ^{(\bar{x})}\in \Gamma TTM$$\end{document}∂μ(x¯)∈ΓTTM and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}\in \Gamma TTM$$\end{document}∂μ(x˙)∈ΓTTM. In induced coordinates, the scalar lift of a scalar field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}h∈ΓΛ0M is given by12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{h}= \dot{x}^\mu \partial ^{(x)}_\mu h. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h˙=x˙μ∂μ(x)h.In general, we will not distinguish between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{x}$$\end{document}x¯ and x unless necessary.

When convenient, the exterior product of coordinate differential forms will be written13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} dx^0\wedge \cdots \wedge dx^{n-1} = dx^{0,\ldots ,n-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dx0∧⋯∧dxn-1=dx0,…,n-1.

Notation and conventions specific for this article

Let M be a spacetime manifold and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi :TM\rightarrow M$$\end{document}π:TM→M, the tangent bundle over M. The slit tangent bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\breve{T}M=TM\backslash \{0\}$$\end{document}T˘M=TM\{0} is the subset of TM which excludes the zero vectors.

Definition 1.5

(Conic Sub-Bundle) A conic sub-bundle is a subset of the slit tangent bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U\subset \breve{T}M$$\end{document}U⊂T˘M, which satisfies the following properties: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi (U)=M$$\end{document}π(U)=MIf \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}λu_∈U.We also use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi$$\end{document}π to also denote the projection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi :U\rightarrow M$$\end{document}π:U→M. A formal discussion of conic bundles can be found in [18]. We additionally assume the properties necessary for U to have a smooth sub-bundle structure.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi (U)=M$$\end{document}π(U)=M

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi (U)=M$$\end{document}π(U)=M

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}λu_∈U.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}λu_∈U.

Definition 1.6

(Causal Indicator) We say U is time-orientable if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U=U^+\cup U^-$$\end{document}U=U+∪U- where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U^+\cap U^-=\varnothing$$\end{document}U+∩U-=∅ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U^+$$\end{document}u_∈U+ if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\underline{u}\in U^-$$\end{document}-u_∈U-. We call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U^+$$\end{document}u_∈U+ future pointing. The causal indicator is the scalar field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in \Gamma \Lambda ^0U$$\end{document}σ∈ΓΛ0U given by14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma \vert _{\underline{u}}= {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1, & \text{ if } \underline{u}\in U^+ \\ -1, & \text{ if } \underline{u}\in U^-. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σ|u_=1,ifu_∈U+-1,ifu_∈U-.

Examples of structures described by conic bundles include the collection of time-like vectors over a spacetime manifold, the light cone, and the causal region of a time-orientable manifold. In the case where we consider only the time-like vectors, the corresponding conic bundle has an open sub-bundle structure. In the case where we consider the causally connected regions of spacetime (the former case plus the light cone), the corresponding conic bundle is a sub-bundle with a boundary. However, it is not a closed set since it excludes the zero vectors.

An important example of a conic bundle which is not time-orientable is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U=\breve{T}M$$\end{document}U=T˘M, for any manifold of dimension greater than 1. This is because each fibre, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\breve{T_p}M$$\end{document}Tp˘M, is connected.

As discussed in the introduction, when dealing with kinematic domains we restrict our attention to time-orientable conic bundles. By contrast, when dealing with the Vlasov bivector approach, we do not have to make this restriction. Thus, the bivector approach can be applied to the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U=\breve{T}M$$\end{document}U=T˘M.

Definition 1.7

(Radial Vector Field) The radial vector field, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}R∈ΓTU, is the unique vector field that satisfies the following condition: for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {R}\langle \pi ^*h\rangle =0, \quad \text{ and } \quad \mathcal {R}\langle \dot{h} \rangle \vert _{\underline{u}}= \dot{h}\vert _{\underline{u}}= \underline{u}\langle h \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R⟨π∗h⟩=0,andR⟨h˙⟩|u_=h˙|u_=u_⟨h⟩.In local induced coordinates, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}$$\end{document}R is given by16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R=x˙μ∂μ(x˙).This can be seen by acting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\langle \dot{x}^\mu \rangle = \dot{x}^\mu$$\end{document}R⟨x˙μ⟩=x˙μ. Within the literature, the radial vector field is known by many names, perhaps the most common of which is the vertical vector field. We have opted to refer to it as the radial vector field as it exhibits many useful radial properties.

Definition 1.8

(Homogeneity) A scalar field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0U$$\end{document}G∈ΓΛ0U is said to be homogeneous of degree k (also known as radially homogeneous or fibre-wise homogeneous) if17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G\vert _{\lambda {\underline{u}}}= \lambda ^k G\vert _{\underline{u}} \quad \text {for all}\quad \lambda \in \mathbb {R}\setminus \{ 0 \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G|λu_=λkG|u_for allλ∈R\{0}.

Note that by Euler’s theorem of homogeneous functions, a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0U$$\end{document}G∈ΓΛ0U is (fibre-wise) k-homogeneous if and only if it satisfies18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {R}\langle G\rangle =kG. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R⟨G⟩=kG.

Vlasov systems and kinematic domains

Kinematic domains and the Vlasov picture

In order to discuss the Vlasov field, we must first establish the space over which it is defined. We call such submanifolds kinematic domains. For this section, we assume that U is time-orientable in the sense of Definition 1.6, and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\subset U^+$$\end{document}E⊂U+, that is all points in E are future pointing. As stated in the introduction, this constraint is not needed for the generalisation to Vlasov bivectors. We assume that each spacetime manifold M (and hence TM) is connected.

We refer to two types of Vlasov fields throughout this paper: Vlasov fields on kinematic domains (denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E\in \Gamma TE$$\end{document}WE∈ΓTE for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\subset U$$\end{document}E⊂U) and Vlasov fields on U (denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU).

Definition 2.2

Given a particle density function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0E$$\end{document}fE∈ΓΛ0E, the Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E\in \Gamma TE$$\end{document}WE∈ΓTE satisfies Eq. (1). The flow of the Vlasov field defines a set of integral curves which trace out the paths of particles through phase space (see Sect. 2.3). The projections of these integral curves into the base space M are the trajectories of the particles described by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_E$$\end{document}fE.

An example of the Vlasov field on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH is the Lorentz force Vlasov equation. Definition 2.3

(Vlasov Field on U) Denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU, a Vlasov field on U is a vector field with the following defining properties: W is horizontal, 26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} W is horizontal, 26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi _*(W\vert _{\underline{u}})=\underline{u}, \text{ or } \text{ equivalently, } W\langle \pi ^*h \rangle =\dot{h}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}π∗(W|u_)=u_,orequivalently,W⟨π∗h⟩=h˙, for any point vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}h∈ΓΛ0M;

Lemma 2.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU be horizontal. Then W is radially quadratic and hence a Vlasov field if and only if29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} [\mathcal {R},W]=W. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[R,W]=W.

Proof

Let W be radially quadratic then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}f∈ΓΛ0M, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\langle \dot{f} \rangle$$\end{document}W⟨f˙⟩ is a 2-homogeneous scalar field. It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\langle W\langle \dot{f}\rangle \rangle = 2W\langle \dot{f}\rangle$$\end{document}R⟨W⟨f˙⟩⟩=2W⟨f˙⟩ by Eq. (28). Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {R},W]\langle \dot{f}\rangle = \mathcal {R}\langle W\langle \dot{f}\rangle \rangle -W\langle \mathcal {R}\langle \dot{f}\rangle \rangle = W\langle \dot{f}\rangle$$\end{document}[R,W]⟨f˙⟩=R⟨W⟨f˙⟩⟩-W⟨R⟨f˙⟩⟩=W⟨f˙⟩. From Eq. (26), we also have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {R},W]\langle \pi ^*f\rangle =\mathcal {R}\langle \dot{f} \rangle = \dot{f} =W\langle \pi ^*f\rangle , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[R,W]⟨π∗f⟩=R⟨f˙⟩=f˙=W⟨π∗f⟩,and hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {R},W]=W$$\end{document}[R,W]=W.

Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {R},W]=W$$\end{document}[R,W]=W, then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}f∈ΓΛ0M we have30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W\langle \dot{f} \rangle = [\mathcal {R},W]\langle \dot{f}\rangle = \mathcal {R}\langle W\langle \dot{f}\rangle \rangle - W\langle \dot{f}\rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W⟨f˙⟩=[R,W]⟨f˙⟩=R⟨W⟨f˙⟩⟩-W⟨f˙⟩.Rearranging the above gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\langle W\langle \dot{f}\rangle \rangle = 2W\langle \dot{f}\rangle$$\end{document}R⟨W⟨f˙⟩⟩=2W⟨f˙⟩. Hence, W is radially quadratic. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Figure 3 shows two Vlasov fields on U that represent the same trajectories on M, just with different parametrisations. One is tangent to the unit hyperboloid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_H$$\end{document}EH while the other is tangent to a lab-time bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{t}$$\end{document}Et. In Sect. 2.4, we see the formula for transforming from one Vlasov field to another in such a way that the particle trajectories are unaffected.

The Vlasov field on U can be written in local coordinates as31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W= \dot{x}^\mu \partial ^{(x)}_\mu +\varphi ^\mu \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W=x˙μ∂μ(x)+φμ∂μ(x˙),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^\mu$$\end{document}φμ are 2-homogeneous scalar fields, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^\mu \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}=\lambda ^2\varphi ^\mu \vert _{\underline{u}}$$\end{document}φμ|λu_=λ2φμ|u_. The Vlasov field W on U can be reduced to a Vlasov field on E provided it is tangent to it.

Definition 2.5

(Extension of a Vlasov Field on E) Given a kinematic domain E with inclusion map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _E:E\hookrightarrow U$$\end{document}ΣE:E↪U and a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E\in \Gamma TE$$\end{document}WE∈ΓTE, we call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU the extension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE if W is tangent to E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE is induced by W, as in Eq. (7),32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Sigma _{E}{}_*\left( W_{E}\vert _{\underline{v}} \right) = W \vert _{\Sigma _E(\underline{v})}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΣE∗WE|v_=W|ΣE(v_),for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E$$\end{document}v_∈E.

Lemma 2.6

Let W be a Vlasov field and G be any k-homogeneous scalar field. Then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W\vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}\langle G\rangle = \lambda ^{k+1} W\vert _{\underline{u}}\langle G\rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W|λu_⟨G⟩=λk+1W|u_⟨G⟩.

Proof

Let G be a k-homogeneous function. First note that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}\left( G\vert _{\lambda \underline{u}} \right) = \lambda \left( \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})} G \right) \big \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂μ(x˙)G|λu_=λ∂μ(x˙)G|λu_,by the chain rule. Hence, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \left( \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})} G \right) \big \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}} = \lambda ^{-1} \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}\left( G\vert _{\lambda \underline{u}} \right) = \lambda ^{-1} \partial ^{(\dot{x})}_\mu \left( \lambda ^k G\vert _{\underline{u}} \right) = \lambda ^{k-1} \left( \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})} G \right) \big \vert _{\underline{u}}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂μ(x˙)G|λu_=λ-1∂μ(x˙)G|λu_=λ-1∂μ(x˙)λkG|u_=λk-1∂μ(x˙)G|u_.We also have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \partial _\mu ^{(x)}G \right) \big \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}= \partial _\mu ^{(x)}\left( \lambda ^k G\vert _{\underline{u}} \right) =\lambda ^k \left( \partial _\mu ^{(x)}G \right) \big \vert _{\underline{u}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂μ(x)G|λu_=∂μ(x)λkG|u_=λk∂μ(x)G|u_.By expanding the Vlasov field in coordinates, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} W\vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}\langle G\rangle&= \left( \dot{x}^\mu \partial _\mu ^{(x)}G+ \varphi ^\mu \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}G \right) \big \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}} = \lambda \underline{u} \lambda ^k \left( \partial _\mu ^{(x)}G \right) \big \vert _{\underline{u}} +\lambda ^2 \varphi ^\mu \vert _{\underline{u}} \lambda ^{k-1} \left( \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})} G \right) \big \vert _{\underline{u}} \\{}&= \lambda ^{k+1} \left( \dot{x}^\mu \partial _\mu ^{(x)}G+ \varphi ^\mu \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}G \right) \big \vert _{\underline{u}} = \lambda ^{k+1} W\vert _{\underline{u}} \langle G\rangle , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W|λu_⟨G⟩=x˙μ∂μ(x)G+φμ∂μ(x˙)G|λu_=λu_λk∂μ(x)G|u_+λ2φμ|u_λk-1∂μ(x˙)G|u_=λk+1x˙μ∂μ(x)G+φμ∂μ(x˙)G|u_=λk+1W|u_⟨G⟩,hence the result. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

It is worth noting at this point that what we call Vlasov fields on U are referred to as sprays and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE are referred to as semi-sprays in literature (see [26] or [27] for an overview). The correspondence between these objects is explored in Sect. A.1.

We now give the example of the Lorentz force equation and the corresponding Vlasov fields adapted to the unit hyperbolid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH and the lab bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{t}$$\end{document}Et. Here U is the conic bundle of time-like vectors. The Vlasov field on U adapted to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH is given by33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W=\dot{x}^\mu \partial ^{(x)}_\mu + \left( \frac{q}{m} \sigma \sqrt{ F_{\text {H}}} g^{\mu \nu }\mathcal {F}_{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\rho - \Gamma ^\mu _{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\nu \dot{x}^\rho \right) \partial ^{(\dot{x})}_\mu , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W=x˙μ∂μ(x)+qmσFHgμνFνρx˙ρ-Γνρμx˙νx˙ρ∂μ(x˙),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}FH is as defined in Eq. (20). The inclusion of the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \sqrt{F_{\text {H}}}$$\end{document}σFH is to ensure the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^{(\dot{x})}_\mu$$\end{document}∂μ(x˙) term is 2-homogeneous and future pointing. The parameterisation associated with the trajectories of this Vlasov field is proper time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document}τ. The Vlasov field on U describing particles subject to the Lorentz force in the lab-time bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{E_{t}}$$\end{document}WEt with associated lab time t is given by34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \hspace{-0.6em} \hat{W}&= \dot{x}^\mu \partial ^{(x)}_\mu + \bigg ( \frac{q}{m} \sigma \sqrt{ F_{\text {H}}} g^{\mu \nu }\mathcal {F}_{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\rho -\Gamma ^\mu _{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\nu \dot{x}^\rho \bigg ) \partial ^{(\dot{x})}_\mu \\{}&\quad - \bigg ( \frac{q}{m} \sigma \sqrt{F_{\text {H}}} g^{\lambda \nu }\mathcal {F}_{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\rho \frac{\partial t}{\partial x^\lambda } - \Gamma ^\lambda _{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\nu \dot{x}^\rho \frac{\partial t}{\partial x^\lambda } +\dot{x}^\nu \dot{x}^\rho \frac{\partial t}{\partial x^\nu \partial x^\rho } \bigg ) \frac{\dot{x}^\mu }{\dot{t}} \partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W^=x˙μ∂μ(x)+(qmσFHgμνFνρx˙ρ-Γνρμx˙νx˙ρ)∂μ(x˙)-(qmσFHgλνFνρx˙ρ∂t∂xλ-Γνρλx˙νx˙ρ∂t∂xλ+x˙νx˙ρ∂t∂xν∂xρ)x˙μt˙∂μ(x˙).When using a lab-time bundle, it is often convenient to choose an adapted coordinate system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x^1,x^2,x^3)$$\end{document}(t,x1,x2,x3) on M. In this coordinate system, Eq. (34) becomes35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \hat{W}&= \dot{x}^\mu \partial ^{(x)}_\mu + \bigg ( \frac{q}{m} \sigma \sqrt{ F_{\text {H}}} g^{\mu \nu }\mathcal {F}_{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\rho -\Gamma ^\mu _{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\nu \dot{x}^\rho \bigg ) \partial ^{(\dot{x})}_\mu \\ - \left( \frac{q}{m} \sigma \sqrt{F_{\text {H}}} g^{0\nu }\mathcal {F}_{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\rho - \Gamma ^0_{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\nu \dot{x}^\rho \right) \frac{\dot{x}^\mu }{\dot{t}}\partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W^=x˙μ∂μ(x)+(qmσFHgμνFνρx˙ρ-Γνρμx˙νx˙ρ)∂μ(x˙)-qmσFHg0νFνρx˙ρ-Γνρ0x˙νx˙ρx˙μt˙∂μ(x˙),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x^0=t$$\end{document}x0=t. We see in Sect. 2.6 that we can demonstrate the transformation between Eqs. (33) and (34) using the underlying second-order ODEs as done in Lemma 2.17. However, it is much easier to calculate these transformations after we have defined the kinematic indicator in the following subsection. This leads to the main result of this section in Theorem 2.18.

Kinematic indicators

A kinematic domain can be defined in terms of a scalar field on U, and we call this field a kinematic indicator.

Definition 2.7

(Kinematic Indicator) A kinematic indicator for E is a non-vanishing scalar field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0 U$$\end{document}F∈ΓΛ0U with nonzero integer degree of homogeneity k, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\vert _{\lambda \underline{v}}=\lambda ^k F\vert _{\underline{v}}$$\end{document}F|λv_=λkF|v_, such that F is positively valued on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U^+$$\end{document}U+, and E is given by36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E=\{ \underline{u}\in U :F\vert _{\underline{u}}=a \; \text{ and } \; \sigma \vert _{\underline{u}}=1\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E={u_∈U:F|u_=aandσ|u_=1},for some positive number a. Note that if k is odd, then the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \vert _{\underline{u}}=1$$\end{document}σ|u_=1 becomes redundant.

For example, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}FH given in Eq. (20) is a kinematic indicator for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH, the unit hyperboloid. A second example \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F=\dot{t}$$\end{document}F=t˙ is a kinematic indicator for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{t}$$\end{document}Et, the lab-time bundle.

Lemma 2.8

Given an arbitrary kinematic domain E, there exists a unique 1-homogeneous kinematic indicator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0 U$$\end{document}F∈ΓΛ0U such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=\{ \underline{u}\in U: F\vert _{\underline{u}}=1 \}.$$\end{document}E={u_∈U:F|u_=1}. For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U,$$\end{document}u_∈U, this is given by37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F\vert _{\underline{u}}=\lambda \quad \text{ where } \;\underline{u}=\lambda \underline{v}\; \text{ for } \text{ a } \text{ unique } \underline{v}\in E. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F|u_=λwhereu_=λv_forauniquev_∈E.

Proof

For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U, there exists a unique \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}λ∈R\{0} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E$$\end{document}v_∈E such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}=\lambda \underline{v}$$\end{document}u_=λv_ by the conic properties of U (Definition 1.5). We define then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\vert _{\underline{u}}=\lambda$$\end{document}F|u_=λ. Since each component of E is connected, F is smooth, and hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0 U$$\end{document}F∈ΓΛ0U. To see that F is unique, suppose there is another scalar \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F'$$\end{document}F′ which is 1-homogeneous and is such that such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=\{ \underline{u}\in U: F'\vert _{\underline{u}}=1 \}$$\end{document}E={u_∈U:F′|u_=1} and pick any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U. Then by the 1-homogeneity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F'$$\end{document}F′, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F'\vert _{\underline{u}}=F'\vert _{\lambda \underline{v}}=\lambda =F\vert _{\underline{u}}$$\end{document}F′|u_=F′|λv_=λ=F|u_. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Given a kinematic indicator F for a kinematic domain E as defined in Lemma 2.8, we may define another scalar for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}k∈Z, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ne 0$$\end{document}k≠0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}a∈R+ by38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{F}= aF^k. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F^=aFk.Not only is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ a k-homogeneous kinematic indicator for E, it is also unique for the chosen k and a.

Lemma 2.9

Let F be a kinematic indicator for E as given by Lemma 2.8 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ be given by Eq. (38). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ is a kinematic indicator for E.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{E}$$\end{document}E^ be a contour of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ as given by Eq. (36). For and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E$$\end{document}v_∈E, notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^k\vert _{\underline{v}}=1$$\end{document}Fk|v_=1 so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}\vert _{\underline{v}}=aF^k\vert _{\underline{v}}=a$$\end{document}F^|v_=aFk|v_=a. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E$$\end{document}v_∈E implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in \hat{E}$$\end{document}v_∈E^. The converse can be proved similarly to show \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in \hat{E}$$\end{document}v_∈E^ implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E$$\end{document}v_∈E. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ is a kinematic indicator for E. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Lemma 2.10

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}a∈R+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ is a k-homogeneous kinematic indicator for E such that Eq. (36) is satisfied, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ is uniquely given by Eq. (38).

Proof

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ satisfies the following properties:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{F}\vert _{\underline{v}}=a \quad \text {and}\quad \hat{F}\vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}= \lambda ^k \hat{F}\vert _{\underline{u}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F^|v_=aandF^|λu_=λkF^|u_,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E$$\end{document}v_∈E, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0. These two functions can be related by39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{F}\vert _{\underline{v}}= a =a F\vert _{\underline{v}}, \quad \text { and } \quad \frac{(\sigma \hat{F})\vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}}{\hat{F}\vert _{\underline{u}}}= \sigma \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}\lambda ^k=\sigma \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}\left( \frac{F\vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}}{F\vert _{\underline{u}}} \right) ^k, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F^|v_=a=aF|v_,and(σF^)|λu_F^|u_=σ|λu_λk=σ|λu_F|λu_F|u_k,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E,\;\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}v_∈E,u_∈U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0.

By the conic property, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U there exists a unique \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E$$\end{document}v_∈E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}=\lambda \underline{v}$$\end{document}u_=λv_. By plugging such a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}$$\end{document}v_ into Eq. (39), we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\hat{F}\vert _{\lambda \underline{v}}}{a}= \left( \frac{F\vert _{\lambda \underline{v}}}{1} \right) ^k \implies \hat{F}\vert _{\underline{u}}= aF^k\vert _{\underline{u}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F^|λv_a=F|λv_1k⇒F^|u_=aFk|u_.Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}=aF^k$$\end{document}F^=aFk. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

In the case of the upper unit hyperboloid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}FH [Eq. (20)], the kinematic indicator is 2-homogeneous.
