The equivalent 1-homogeneous kinematic indicator is given by40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{F}_H\vert _{\underline{u}}= \sigma \vert _{\underline{u}} \sqrt{F_{\text {H}}\vert _{\underline{u}}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F^H|u_=σ|u_FH|u_.where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document}σ is given by Definition 1.6.

At this point, we may observe some similarities with foliations of the tangent bundle based on Finsler metrics [28]. The function F which defines E can be a Finsler metric; however, it is not a necessary condition. The only requirement for F is that it be homogeneous of some degree.

Definition 2.11

(Compatible Vlasov Field) Given a kinematic indicator F with associated kinematic domain E, a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU is said to be compatible with F if it satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\langle F\rangle =0$$\end{document}W⟨F⟩=0. The restriction of W to a compatible E is then denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E\in \Gamma TE$$\end{document}WE∈ΓTE as given by eq. (32).

Note that by Lemma A.5, W is tangent to a kinematic domain E with kinematic indicator F if and only if W is compatible with F.

Lemma 2.12

Given a kinematic domain E and a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E\in \Gamma TE$$\end{document}WE∈ΓTE, there exists a unique Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU which is an extension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE, or equivalently \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE is induced by W.

Proof

First observe that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}f∈ΓΛ0M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E$$\end{document}v_∈E,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \dot{f}\circ \Sigma _E \right) \vert _{\underline{v}}= \dot{f}\vert _{\Sigma _E(\underline{v})}=\dot{f}\vert _{\underline{v}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f˙∘ΣE|v_=f˙|ΣE(v_)=f˙|v_.It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{f}\circ \Sigma _E= \dot{f}$$\end{document}f˙∘ΣE=f˙.

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}f∈ΓΛ0M, define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi _*(W\vert _{\underline{u}}) = \underline{u} \; \text{ and } \; W\vert _{\underline{u}}\langle \dot{f}\rangle = \lambda ^2 W_E\vert _{\underline{v}}\langle \dot{f}\rangle , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}π∗(W|u_)=u_andW|u_⟨f˙⟩=λ2WE|v_⟨f˙⟩,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}=\lambda \underline{v}$$\end{document}u_=λv_ by the conic property for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in E$$\end{document}v_∈E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0. By construction, W is radially quadratic and horizontal so it remains to show that it is unique.

Let both W and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ induce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE as defined above and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=\hat{W}-W$$\end{document}X=W^-W. Observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _*X=0$$\end{document}π∗X=0 and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X\vert _{\underline{u}}\langle \dot{f}\rangle = \lambda ^2(W_E-W_E)\vert _{\underline{v}}\langle \dot{f}\rangle =0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X|u_⟨f˙⟩=λ2(WE-WE)|v_⟨f˙⟩=0.Hence, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=0$$\end{document}X=0 and W is unique.

To see that W is compatible with F (and hence tangent to E by Lemma A.5), consider the following. Without loss of generality, we may assume F is 1-homogeneous. By Lemma 2.6, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\vert _{\underline{u}}\langle F\rangle = \lambda ^2 W\vert _{\underline{v}}\langle F\rangle$$\end{document}W|u_⟨F⟩=λ2W|v_⟨F⟩. It follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} W\vert _{\underline{u}}\langle F\rangle&= \lambda ^2 W\vert _{\underline{v}} \langle F\rangle = \lambda ^2 (\Sigma _E{}_*W_E\vert _{\underline{v}}) \langle F\rangle = \lambda ^2 W_E\vert _{\underline{v}} \langle F\circ \Sigma _E \rangle = \lambda ^2 W_E\vert _{\underline{v}} \langle 1 \rangle =0. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W|u_⟨F⟩=λ2W|v_⟨F⟩=λ2(ΣE∗WE|v_)⟨F⟩=λ2WE|v_⟨F∘ΣE⟩=λ2WE|v_⟨1⟩=0.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Trajectories, prolongations, and the horizontal condition on U

The notion of integral curves for Vlasov fields on kinematic domains can be extended to integral curves on U. The Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in\Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTUgenerates a set of integral curves on U, denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η. The projections of these curves onto M are exactly the trajectories of the particles and are denoted C. Since we restrict our attention here to integral curves of horizontal vector fields, the terms prolongation and integral curve can be used interchangeably.

Trajectories can be considered in terms of maps from intervals of the real line \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R into the spacetime manifold M:41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C:\mathcal {I}\hookrightarrow M. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C:I↪M.These trajectories can be parameterised by choosing a parameter, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}s∈ΓΛ0I with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ds\ne 0$$\end{document}ds≠0. We then write the parameterised trajectories as C(s).

Definition 2.13

(Prolongations) For some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}s∈ΓΛ0I and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _s\in \Gamma T\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}∂s∈ΓTI, the prolongation of a curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C:\mathcal {I}\hookrightarrow M$$\end{document}C:I↪M is given by42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \eta :\mathcal {I}\hookrightarrow U,\; \eta (s_0)=C_*(\partial _s\vert _{s_0}),\; \forall s_0\in \mathcal {I}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}η:I↪U,η(s0)=C∗(∂s|s0),∀s0∈I.

Lemma 2.14

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta :\mathcal {I}\hookrightarrow U$$\end{document}η:I↪U is the prolongation of some trajectory C if and only if43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\pi \circ \eta )_*(\partial _s)=\eta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(π∘η)∗(∂s)=η.

Proof

First, suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η is the prolongation of C. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_*(\partial _s|_{s_0})\in T_{C(s_0)}M$$\end{document}C∗(∂s|s0)∈TC(s0)M. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi \big (\eta (s_0)\big ) = \pi \big (C_*(\partial _s|_{s_0})\big )=C(s)$$\end{document}π(η(s0))=π(C∗(∂s|s0))=C(s), i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi \circ \eta =C,$$\end{document}π∘η=C, and hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\pi \circ \eta )_*(\partial _s)=C_*(\partial _s)=\eta$$\end{document}(π∘η)∗(∂s)=C∗(∂s)=η.

Conversely, assuming Eq. (43), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=\pi \circ \eta$$\end{document}C=π∘η, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_*(\partial _s) = (\pi \circ \eta )_*(\partial _s) = \eta$$\end{document}C∗(∂s)=(π∘η)∗(∂s)=η. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mathcal {I}}\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I^⊂R be coordinated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}$$\end{document}s^ and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{C}:\hat{\mathcal {I}}\hookrightarrow M$$\end{document}C^:I^↪M be an alternative parameterisation of C. That is, there exists a diffeomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}=\hat{s}(s)$$\end{document}s^=s^(s) such that44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{C}\big (\hat{s}(s)\big )=C(s). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C^(s^(s))=C(s).Note that although the two parameterisations define the same curve, the prolongations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\eta }}$$\end{document}η^ do not coincide. Consequently, the tangent vectors along the prolongations (i.e. acceleration) belong to different spaces: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _*(\partial _s\vert _{s_0}) \in T_{\eta (s_0)}U$$\end{document}η∗(∂s|s0)∈Tη(s0)U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\eta }_*(\partial _{\hat{s}}\vert _{\hat{s}_0}) \in T_{\hat{\eta }(\hat{s}_0)}U$$\end{document}η^∗(∂s^|s^0)∈Tη^(s^0)U even when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C(s_0)=\hat{C}(s_1)$$\end{document}C(s0)=C^(s1).

Definition 2.15

An integral curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η of W is said to lie along E if45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \eta (s) \in E,\; \forall s\in \mathcal {I}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}η(s)∈E,∀s∈I.This is a necessary condition for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η to be an integral curve of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE is induced by W, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η is an integral curve of W, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η lies along E, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η is also an integral curve of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE.

Lemma 2.16

All integral curves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η of a vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}X∈ΓTU are prolongations if and only if X is horizontal.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η be an integral curve of X, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\vert _{\eta (s_1)}= \eta _*(\partial _s\vert _{s_1})$$\end{document}X|η(s1)=η∗(∂s|s1) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_1\in \mathcal {I}$$\end{document}s1∈I, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (s_0)=\underline{u}$$\end{document}η(s0)=u_ for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_0\in \mathcal {I}$$\end{document}s0∈I.

Suppose first that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η is a prolongation. Then46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \pi _*X\vert _{\underline{u}}&= \pi _*X\vert _{\eta (s_0)} = \pi _*\eta _*(\partial _s\vert _{s_0}) = (\pi \circ \eta )_*(\partial _s\vert _{s_0})= \eta (s_0)= \underline{u}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}π∗X|u_=π∗X|η(s0)=π∗η∗(∂s|s0)=(π∘η)∗(∂s|s0)=η(s0)=u_.Hence, X is horizontal.

Suppose now that X is horizontal. We have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _*X\vert _{\underline{u}}= \underline{u}= \eta (s_0)$$\end{document}π∗X|u_=u_=η(s0). Then,47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \eta (s_0)= \pi _*X\vert _{\eta (s_0)} = \pi _*\eta _*(\partial _s\vert _{s_0}) =(\pi \circ \eta )_*(\partial _s\vert _{s_0}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}η(s0)=π∗X|η(s0)=π∗η∗(∂s|s0)=(π∘η)∗(∂s|s0).Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η is a prolongation. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

The integral curves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η of Vlasov fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU correspond to trajectories C in the base space M through the following relation:48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C=\pi \circ \eta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C=π∘η.In local coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^\mu ,\dot{x}^\mu ),$$\end{document}(xμ,x˙μ), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\mu (s_0)=x^\mu \vert _{C(s_0)}$$\end{document}Cμ(s0)=xμ|C(s0) then49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{C}^\mu (s_0)= \frac{dC^\mu }{ds} \bigg \vert _{s_0} \; \text{ and } \; \ddot{C}^\mu (s_0)= \frac{d^2C^\mu }{ds^2}\bigg \vert _{s_0}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C˙μ(s0)=dCμds|s0andC¨μ(s0)=d2Cμds2|s0.These trajectories can be expressed in terms of a parameterised system of second-order differential equations in terms of the coefficients of W:50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ddot{C}^\mu \vert _{s_0}=\varphi ^\mu \vert _{\dot{C}(s_0)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C¨μ|s0=φμ|C˙(s0),where s is a parameter corresponding to W and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^\mu$$\end{document}φμ are the coefficients of W as given in Eq. (31). Due to the projection in Eq. (48), there is a class of integral curves, with corresponding vector fields, that produce the same trajectories. These vector fields differ only by a term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\mathcal {R}$$\end{document}kR where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0U$$\end{document}k∈ΓΛ0U is a 1-homogeneous function. The converse is also true, and a full proof of this statement can be found in [26]. These vector fields are said to be projectively related in the literature (see Definition A.2 for a definition). We present here a proof of only the former statement to illustrate this result in our mathematical notation.

Lemma 2.17

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W,\hat{W}\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W,W^∈ΓTU be Vlasov fields and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0U$$\end{document}k∈ΓΛ0U be a 1-homogeneous scalar field. If W and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ are related by51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{W}=W+k\mathcal {R} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W^=W+kR,then they have the same trajectories up to a parameterisation. That is, if the trajectories C of W are parameterised by s then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ has the same trajectories C only parameterised by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}$$\end{document}s^ where52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\text {d}^2\hat{s}}{\text {d}s^2}\bigg \vert _{s_0}+ k\vert _{\underline{u}} \frac{\text {d}\hat{s}}{\text {d}s}\bigg \vert _{s_0}=0,\; \frac{\text {d}\hat{s}}{\text {d}s}>0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}d2s^ds2|s0+k|u_ds^ds|s0=0,ds^ds>0,and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}=C_*(\partial _s\vert _{s_0})\in U$$\end{document}u_=C∗(∂s|s0)∈U.

Proof

Notice that if W and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ are related by Eq. (51) then in local coordinates we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\varphi }^\mu =\varphi ^\mu + k\dot{x}^\mu , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}φ^μ=φμ+kx˙μ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^\mu$$\end{document}φμ (resp. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\varphi }^\mu$$\end{document}φ^μ) are the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu ^{(\dot{x})}$$\end{document}∂μ(x˙) coefficients of W (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^), see eq. (31). Let the trajectories of W be denoted by C(s). These trajectories satisfy Eq. (50). Define a new parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}=\hat{s}(s)$$\end{document}s^=s^(s) by Eq. (52) and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}_0=\hat{s}(s_0)$$\end{document}s^0=s^(s0).

The trajectory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C(\hat{s}(s))$$\end{document}C(s^(s)) can be shown to satisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\text {d}^2C^\mu }{\text {d}s^2}\bigg \vert _{s_0}= \frac{\text {d}^2\hat{s}}{\text {d}s^2}\bigg \vert _{s_0}\frac{\text {d}C^\mu }{\text {d}\hat{s}}\bigg \vert _{\hat{s}_0} +\left( \frac{\text {d}\hat{s}}{\text {d}s}\bigg \vert _{s_0} \right) ^2 \frac{\text {d}^2C^\mu }{\text {d}\hat{s}^2}\bigg \vert _{\hat{s}_0}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}d2Cμds2|s0=d2s^ds2|s0dCμds^|s^0+ds^ds|s02d2Cμds^2|s^0.We therefore have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \frac{\text {d}^2C^\mu }{\text {d}\hat{s}^2}&\bigg \vert _{\hat{s}_0}= \left( \frac{\text {d}\hat{s}}{\text {d}s}\bigg \vert _{s_0} \right) ^{-2} \! \left( \frac{\text {d}^2C^\mu }{\text {d}s^2}\bigg \vert _{s_0}- \frac{\text {d}^2\hat{s}}{\text {d}s^2}\bigg \vert _{s_0} \frac{\text {d}C^\mu }{\text {d}\hat{s}}\bigg \vert _{\hat{s}_0} \right) = \left( \frac{\text {d}\hat{s}}{\text {d}s}\bigg \vert _{s_0} \right) ^{-2} \bigg ( \varphi ^\mu \vert _{C_*(\partial _s\vert _{s_0})} +k\vert _{C_*(\partial _s\vert _{s_0})} \frac{\text {d}\hat{s}}{\text {d}s}\bigg \vert _{s_0} \frac{\text {d}C^\mu }{\text {d}\hat{s}}\bigg \vert _{\hat{s}_0} \bigg )\\ {}&= \varphi ^\mu \vert _{\underline{\hat{u}}}+ k\vert _{\underline{\hat{u}}} \frac{\text {d}dC^\mu }{\text {d}\hat{s}}\bigg \vert _{\hat{s}_0} = \left( \varphi ^\mu +k\dot{x}^\mu \right) \vert _{\underline{\hat{u}}} = \hat{\varphi }^\mu \vert _{\underline{\hat{u}}}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}d2Cμds^2|s^0=ds^ds|s0-2d2Cμds2|s0-d2s^ds2|s0dCμds^|s^0=ds^ds|s0-2(φμ|C∗(∂s|s0)+k|C∗(∂s|s0)ds^ds|s0dCμds^|s^0)=φμ|u^_+k|u^_ddCμds^|s^0=φμ+kx˙μ|u^_=φ^μ|u^_,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\hat{u}}=C_*(\partial _{\hat{s}}\vert _{\hat{s}_0})$$\end{document}u^_=C∗(∂s^|s^0). The third line is due to the 2-homogeneity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^\mu$$\end{document}φμ and 1-homogeneity of k. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C(\hat{s})$$\end{document}C(s^) are the trajectories associated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^. Hence, both W and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ have the same trajectories up to a reparameterisation. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

In the case of special relativity, with local coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x^a)$$\end{document}(t,xa), where t is a lab-time coordinate and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=t$$\end{document}s=t, the Lorentz force equation may be written in velocity coordinates as53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v^0=1,\quad \frac{\text {d}\vec {v}}{\text {d}t} = \frac{1}{\gamma } \frac{q}{m} \left( \vec {E} + \vec {v} \times \vec {B} \right) -\frac{1}{\gamma } \frac{q}{m} \left( \vec {v}\cdot \vec {E} \right) \vec {v}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v0=1,dv→dt=1γqmE→+v→×B→-1γqmv→·E→v→.where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {v}$$\end{document}v→ is the 3-velocity of the particle and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document}γ is the Lorentz factor. We see that in this case the new (second) term in Eq. (51) correspond to the second term in Eq. (53), which arises from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\gamma }{dt}$$\end{document}dγdt.

Transforming between kinematic domains

Suppose we are given a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE on a kinematic domain E with kinematic indicator F. Using this data, we can construct a new Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ which is compatible with a new kinematic indicator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ with associated kinematic domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{E}$$\end{document}E^. This defines a new Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}_{\hat{E}}$$\end{document}W^E^ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{E}$$\end{document}E^ which produces the same trajectories in the base space M as the initial Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE.

Once we have promoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E$$\end{document}WE into a Vlasov field W on U using Lemma 2.12, we can use it to construct a new Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ which is tangent to another kinematic domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{E}$$\end{document}E^. A visualisation of the following lemma is shown in Fig. 6.

Theorem 2.18

Let E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{E}$$\end{document}E^ be kinematic domains with kinematic indicator F and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^, respectively. Given a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU which is compatible with F, we may construct a new Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W^∈ΓTU given by54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{W}=W-\frac{W\langle \hat{F}\rangle }{\mathcal {R}\langle \hat{F}\rangle } \mathcal {R} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W^=W-W⟨F^⟩R⟨F^⟩Rwhich is compatible with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{F}$$\end{document}F^ and corresponds to the same trajectories as W up to parameterisation.

Proof

It is clear that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}\langle \hat{F}\rangle =0$$\end{document}W^⟨F^⟩=0. To see that the resultant vector field is horizontal, observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \pi _*(\hat{W}\vert _{\underline{u}})&= \pi _*(W\vert _{\underline{u}}) -\frac{W\langle \hat{F}\rangle }{\mathcal {R}\langle \hat{F}\rangle } \bigg \vert _{\underline{u}} \pi _*(\mathcal {R}\vert _{\underline{u}})= \pi _*(W\vert _{\underline{u}})= \underline{u} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}π∗(W^|u_)=π∗(W|u_)-W⟨F^⟩R⟨F^⟩|u_π∗(R|u_)=π∗(W|u_)=u_To see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ is radially quadratic, observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \hat{W}\vert _{\lambda \underline{v}} \langle \dot{h}\rangle&= W\vert _{\lambda \underline{v}} \langle \dot{h}\rangle - \frac{W\langle \hat{F}\rangle }{\mathcal {R}\langle \hat{F}\rangle } \bigg \vert _{\lambda \underline{v}} \mathcal {R}\vert _{\lambda \underline{v}} \langle \dot{h}\rangle = \lambda ^2 W\vert _{\underline{v}} \langle \dot{h}\rangle - \frac{\lambda ^{k+1}W\vert _{\underline{v}}\langle \hat{F}\rangle }{\lambda ^k \mathcal {R}\vert _{\underline{v}} \langle \hat{F}\rangle } (\lambda \underline{v} \langle \dot{h}\rangle )\\&= \lambda ^2 \bigg ( W\vert _{\underline{v}} \langle \dot{h}\rangle - \frac{W \langle \hat{F}\rangle }{ \mathcal {R} \langle \hat{F}\rangle }\bigg \vert _{\underline{v}} \mathcal {R}\vert _{\underline{v}} \langle \dot{h}\rangle \bigg ) = \lambda ^2 \hat{W}\vert _{\underline{v}} \langle \dot{h} \rangle , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W^|λv_⟨h˙⟩=W|λv_⟨h˙⟩-W⟨F^⟩R⟨F^⟩|λv_R|λv_⟨h˙⟩=λ2W|v_⟨h˙⟩-λk+1W|v_⟨F^⟩λkR|v_⟨F^⟩(λv_⟨h˙⟩)=λ2(W|v_⟨h˙⟩-W⟨F^⟩R⟨F^⟩|v_R|v_⟨h˙⟩)=λ2W^|v_⟨h˙⟩,for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0 M$$\end{document}h∈ΓΛ0M, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{v}\in U$$\end{document}v_∈U, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ is a valid Vlasov field. To see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ corresponds to the same set of trajectories as W, notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\langle \hat{F}\rangle / \mathcal {R}\langle \hat{F}\rangle$$\end{document}W⟨F^⟩/R⟨F^⟩ in Eq. (54) is a 1-homogeneous function. Hence, by 2.17, W and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ have the same trajectories up to parameterisation. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

The transport equations on kinematic domains

A method of interpreting the Vlasov equation in a kinematic domain E can be given in terms of the transport equations [29]. The transport equations are written in terms of a particle density form which codifies the phase space trajectories corresponding to a given Vlasov field. With some additional structure (a choice of non-vanishing top form, otherwise known as a measure), we can recover the usual Vlasov equation (i.e. a particle density function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0 E$$\end{document}fE∈ΓΛ0E such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E\langle f_E \rangle =0$$\end{document}WE⟨fE⟩=0) from the transport equations on our preferred choice of kinetic domain E.

Definition 2.19

(Transport Equations on E) Consider a kinematic domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\subset U$$\end{document}E⊂U, a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2n{-}2)$$\end{document}(2n-2)-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n-2}E$$\end{document}θE∈ΓΛ2n-2E, and a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E\in \Gamma TE$$\end{document}WE∈ΓTE. The transport equations on E are written55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d\theta _E=0,\; i_{W_E}\theta _E=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dθE=0,iWEθE=0.A visualisation of the transport equations is given in Fig. 4. These geometric statements are equivalent to the Vlasov equation under appropriate conditions. We call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E$$\end{document}θE a particle density form on E.

Let E be a kinematic domain, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E\in \Gamma TE$$\end{document}WE∈ΓTE a Vlasov field on E, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n-1}E$$\end{document}ΩE∈ΓΛ2n-1E be a measure on E such that56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_{W_E}\Omega _E=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LWEΩE=0.We can relate a particle density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2n{-}2)$$\end{document}(2n-2)-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n-2}E$$\end{document}θE∈ΓΛ2n-2E and a particle density function (PDF) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0E$$\end{document}fE∈ΓΛ0E via57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _E= f_Ei_{W_E} \Omega _E. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θE=fEiWEΩE.

Lemma 2.20

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n-2}E$$\end{document}θE∈ΓΛ2n-2E such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{W_E}\theta _E=0$$\end{document}iWEθE=0 then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0E$$\end{document}fE∈ΓΛ0E, given by Eq. (57), exists and is unique. Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E$$\end{document}θE satisfies the transport equations Eq. (55) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_E$$\end{document}fE satisfies Eq. (1) that is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_E\langle f_E\rangle =0$$\end{document}WE⟨fE⟩=0.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^0,\ldots ,x^{2n-2})$$\end{document}(x0,…,x2n-2) define a coordinate system on E such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _0^{(x)}=W_E$$\end{document}∂0(x)=WE. Then,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega _E=\Omega _0\text {d}x^0\wedge \cdots \wedge \text {d}x^{2n-2}, \text{ and } \theta _E= \theta _E^\mu i_\mu ^{(x)}\Omega _E, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΩE=Ω0dx0∧⋯∧dx2n-2,andθE=θEμiμ(x)ΩE,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =0,\ldots ,2n-2$$\end{document}μ=0,…,2n-2. We then have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} 0 = i_{W_E}\theta _E&= i_0^{(x)}\theta _E = \theta _E^0 i_0^{(x)}i_0^{(x)} \Omega _E + \theta _E^a i_0^{(x)} i_a^{(x)} \Omega _E = \theta _E^a i_0^{(x)} i_a^{(x)} \Omega _E, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0=iWEθE=i0(x)θE=θE0i0(x)i0(x)ΩE+θEai0(x)ia(x)ΩE=θEai0(x)ia(x)ΩE,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=1,\ldots ,2n-2$$\end{document}a=1,…,2n-2. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E^a=0$$\end{document}θEa=0 for each a. It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E= \theta _E^0i_0^{(x)}\Omega$$\end{document}θE=θE0i0(x)Ω, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E=f_E i_{W_E}\Omega _E$$\end{document}θE=fEiWEΩE.

From Eq. (57), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \text {d}\theta _E&= \text {d}f_E\wedge i_{W_E}\Omega _E= i_{W_E}df \wedge \Omega _E = W_E\langle f_E\rangle \Omega _E; \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dθE=dfE∧iWEΩE=iWEdf∧ΩE=WE⟨fE⟩ΩE;hence, the transport equations for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E$$\end{document}θE are equivalent to Eq. (1). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

As a corollary of Lemma 2.20, the transport equations and the Vlasov equation, Eq. (1), can equivalently be cast as58\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_{W_E}(f_E\Omega _E) = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LWE(fEΩE)=0.An example of the transport equations can be seen in [25]. However, these equations are subtly different in that a form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _E=i_{W_E}\Omega _E$$\end{document}ωE=iWEΩE with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{W_E}\Omega _E =0$$\end{document}LWEΩE=0 is defined and the transport equations are cast as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\omega _E=0$$\end{document}dωE=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{W_E}\omega _E=0$$\end{document}iWEωE=0. Note that the particle density function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_E$$\end{document}fE is absent from this definition.

Given a volume form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n} U$$\end{document}Ω∈ΓΛ2nU on U, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_W\Omega =0$$\end{document}LWΩ=0, and kinematic domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\subset U$$\end{document}E⊂U with inclusion map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _E: E\hookrightarrow U$$\end{document}ΣE:E↪U, the typical choice of top form on E is given by59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega _E= \Sigma _E^*(i_{\mathcal {R}}\Omega ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΩE=ΣE∗(iRΩ).

Lemma 2.21

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n}U$$\end{document}Ω∈ΓΛ2nU such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_W\Omega =0$$\end{document}LWΩ=0. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n-1}E$$\end{document}ΩE∈ΓΛ2n-1E be defined by Eq. (59), then Eq. (56) holds.

Proof

Observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} L_{W_E}\Omega _E&= L_{W_E} \left( \Sigma _E^*i_{\mathcal {R}}\Omega \right) = \Sigma _E^*\left( L_Wi_{\mathcal {R}}\Omega \right) = \Sigma _E^*\left( i_{[W,\mathcal {R}]}\Omega + i_{\mathcal {R}} L_W\Omega \right) = -\Sigma _E^*i_W\Omega = -i_{W_E} \Sigma _E^*\Omega = 0, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LWEΩE=LWEΣE∗iRΩ=ΣE∗LWiRΩ=ΣE∗i[W,R]Ω+iRLWΩ=-ΣE∗iWΩ=-iWEΣE∗Ω=0,since the degree of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega$$\end{document}Ω is greater than the dimension of E. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

In the instance that the chosen kinematic domain is the upper unit hyperboloid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH as given in Eq. (19), then the volume form typically chosen is60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{E_{\text {H}}}= -\frac{\det (g)}{\dot{x}_0} \text {d}x^0\wedge \cdots \wedge \text {d}x^{n-1}\wedge \text {d}\dot{x}^1\wedge \cdots \wedge \text {d}\dot{x}^{n-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΩEH=-det(g)x˙0dx0∧⋯∧dxn-1∧dx˙1∧⋯∧dx˙n-1.Furthermore, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{E_{\text {H}}}$$\end{document}WEH is the Vlasov field corresponding to the Lorentz force it can be shown that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{W_{E_{\text {H}}}}\Omega _{E_{\text {H}}}=0,$$\end{document}LWEHΩEH=0, and hence, the transport equations equipped with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{E_{\text {H}}}$$\end{document}ΩEH are equivalent to the standard Vlasov equation. The choice of volume form in (59) which produces (60) is given by61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega = -\det (g)\, \text {d}x^0\wedge \cdots \wedge \text {d}x^{n-1}\wedge \text {d}\dot{x}^1\wedge \cdots \wedge \text {d}\dot{x}^{n}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω=-det(g)dx0∧⋯∧dxn-1∧dx˙1∧⋯∧dx˙n. It is a straightforward exercise to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_W\Omega =0$$\end{document}LWΩ=0 for the above \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega$$\end{document}Ω and W given by Eq. (33).

It is often the case that we know the measure only on the time slices of E, in Sect. 5.2, for example. In the following lemma, we show that this is sufficient information to specify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega$$\end{document}Ω uniquely. We consider the scalar field s on E to be the time slicing, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}α to be the measure on each time slice. For example, if the particle current density at time s is of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int \dot{x}^\mu \, f_E\, \omega _0\, \text {d}\dot{x}^{1,\cdots ,n-1}$$\end{document}∫x˙μfEω0dx˙1,⋯,n-1, where we integrate over the velocities, then we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\omega _0\, \text {d}\dot{x}^{1,\ldots ,n-1}$$\end{document}α=ω0dx˙1,…,n-1. The other data required is the constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}λ∈R which enables us to extend the uniqueness to the entirety of U. Of course, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega$$\end{document}Ω will depend on the choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}α and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document}λ.

Lemma 2.22

Given an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n-2} E$$\end{document}α∈ΓΛ2n-2E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}λ∈R. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0 E$$\end{document}s∈ΓΛ0E, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ds\ne 0$$\end{document}ds≠0 on E, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{s_0}=\{\underline{v}\in E, s|_{\underline{v}}=s_0\}\subset E$$\end{document}Ss0={v_∈E,s|v_=s0}⊂E and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi _{s_0} :S_{s_0} \rightarrow E$$\end{document}Ξs0:Ss0→E. If there exists an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n} U$$\end{document}Ω∈ΓΛ2nU which satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_W\Omega =0$$\end{document}LWΩ=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\mathcal {R}}\Omega =\lambda \Omega$$\end{document}LRΩ=λΩ, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi _{s_0}^*i_{W_E} \Sigma ^*_E i_{\mathcal {R}}\Omega =\Xi _{s_0}^*\alpha$$\end{document}Ξs0∗iWEΣE∗iRΩ=Ξs0∗α for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_0$$\end{document}s0, then that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega$$\end{document}Ω is unique.

Proof
