Suppose there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ,\hat{\Omega }\in \Gamma \Lambda ^{2n} U$$\end{document}Ω,Ω^∈ΓΛ2nU satisfying the relevant conditions and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =\Omega -\hat{\Omega }$$\end{document}ω=Ω-Ω^. Define a coordinate system on U such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}=\partial ^{(x)}_0,\; W=\partial ^{(x)}_1.$$\end{document}R=∂0(x),W=∂1(x). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =\omega '(x^0,\ldots ,x^{2n-1}) dx^0\wedge \ldots \wedge dx^{2n-1}$$\end{document}ω=ω′(x0,…,x2n-1)dx0∧…∧dx2n-1. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_W\Omega =0$$\end{document}LWΩ=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_W\hat{\Omega }=0$$\end{document}LWΩ^=0 implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_W\omega =0$$\end{document}LWω=0. Furthermore,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \lambda \omega&= L_{\mathcal {R}}\omega = (\partial ^{(x)}_{0}\omega ') \text {d}x^0\wedge \cdots \wedge \text {d}x^{2n-1} + \omega ' L^{(x)}_{0} \text {d}x^0\wedge \cdots \wedge \text {d}x^{2n-1} = (\partial ^{(x)}_{0}\omega ') \text {d}x^0\wedge \cdots \wedge \text {d}x^{2n-1}; \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λω=LRω=(∂0(x)ω′)dx0∧⋯∧dx2n-1+ω′L0(x)dx0∧⋯∧dx2n-1=(∂0(x)ω′)dx0∧⋯∧dx2n-1;hence,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega =\omega '(x^2,\ldots ,x^{n-1}) e^{\lambda x^0} \text {d}x^0\wedge \cdots \wedge \text {d}x^{2n-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ω=ω′(x2,…,xn-1)eλx0dx0∧⋯∧dx2n-1.Also, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi _{s_0}^*i_{W_E} \Sigma ^*_E i_{\mathcal {R}}\Omega =\alpha$$\end{document}Ξs0∗iWEΣE∗iRΩ=α (similarly for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\Omega }$$\end{document}Ω^) for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_0$$\end{document}s0, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi _{s_0}^*i_{W_E} \Sigma _E^*i_{\mathcal {R}}\omega =0$$\end{document}Ξs0∗iWEΣE∗iRω=0; thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega '_0(x^2|_{s_0},\ldots ,x^{n-1}|_{s_0})=0$$\end{document}Ω0′(x2|s0,…,xn-1|s0)=0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_0$$\end{document}s0 so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega '=0,$$\end{document}ω′=0, and hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =0$$\end{document}ω=0. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

We observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega$$\end{document}Ω given by (61) satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\mathcal {R}}\Omega =n \Omega$$\end{document}LRΩ=nΩ. Therefore, by setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=x^0$$\end{document}s=x0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =(-\dot{x}^0 \det g / \dot{x}_0) \text {d}x^{1,\cdots ,n-1} \wedge \text {d}\dot{x}^{1,\cdots ,n-1}$$\end{document}α=(-x˙0detg/x˙0)dx1,⋯,n-1∧dx˙1,⋯,n-1, Lemma 2.22 tells us that Eq. (61) is unique.

There are instances when it is advantageous to cast the Vlasov equation in terms of the transport equations even when the flow of the measure is not preserved by the Vlasov field (i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{W_E}\Omega _E\ne 0$$\end{document}LWEΩE≠0). In [30], an attempt is made to account for the radiation reaction within the Vlasov field over a four-dimensional spacetime manifold M. To accomplish this, the Vlasov equation is cast as62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_{W_Q}(f_Q\omega _Q)=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LWQ(fQωQ)=0.Here the 10-dimensional manifold Q is a subset of the double copy of the tangent bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q\subset TM \oplus TM$$\end{document}Q⊂TM⊕TM where the first copy of the tangent bundle contains normalised time-like velocity vectors (as on the upper unit hyperboloid), and the second copy contains acceleration vectors which are orthogonal to these velocities. The measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _Q\in \Gamma \Lambda ^{10}Q$$\end{document}ωQ∈ΓΛ10Q is formed on Q by pulling back a specific 10 form related to the measure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TM \oplus TM$$\end{document}TM⊕TM. In this case, the flow of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _Q$$\end{document}ωQ along \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_Q$$\end{document}WQ is not conserved:63\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_{W_Q}\omega _Q= \frac{3}{\tau } \omega _Q, \text{ or } \text{ equivalently } W_Q \langle f_Q\rangle + \frac{3}{\tau }f_Q=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LWQωQ=3τωQ,orequivalentlyWQ⟨fQ⟩+3τfQ=0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau =q^2/6\pi m$$\end{document}τ=q2/6πm. The latter equation is similar to Eq. (1), but the additional term on the LHS is to account for losses due to radiation. Note that, although the Vlasov equation is altered, the transport equations remain the same:64\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {d} \theta _Q=0, \quad i_{W_Q}\theta _Q=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dθQ=0,iWQθQ=0,where65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _Q= i_{W_Q}(f_Q\omega _Q). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θQ=iWQ(fQωQ).

Example: the unit hyperboloid and lab-time Vlasov vector fields for the Lorentz force equation

In Eqs. (33), (34), and (35), we gave the Vlasov vector field adapted to the upper unit hyperboloid, an arbitrary time slicing, and a time slicing with respect to coordinate time. We stated that one method to find the transformation is to calculate second-order ODEs, perform the reparameterisation, and then recalculate the corresponding Vlasov vector field, which we do here.

Lemma 2.23

Equation (33) can be transformed into Eq. (34), by reparameterising the Lorentz force ODE.

Proof

Alternatively, we can use the transformation formula Eq. (54).

Lemma 2.24

Equation (33) can be transformed into Eq. (34) and hence Eq. (35), using the transformation formula Eq. (54).

Proof

Follows from69\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \frac{W_{E_{\text {H}}}\left\langle \dot{t} \right\rangle }{\mathcal {R}\left\langle \dot{t} \right\rangle }\mathcal {R}^\mu =&\frac{q}{m} \sqrt{F_{\text {H}}} g^{\lambda \nu }\mathcal {F}_{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\rho \frac{\partial t}{\partial x^\lambda } \frac{\dot{x}^\mu }{\dot{t}} - \Gamma ^\lambda _{\nu \rho }\dot{x}^\nu \dot{x}^\rho \frac{\partial t}{\partial x^\lambda } \frac{\dot{x}^\mu }{\dot{t}} +\dot{x}^\nu \dot{x}^\rho \frac{\partial t}{\partial x^\nu \partial x^\rho } \frac{\dot{x}^\mu }{\dot{t}}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}WEHt˙Rt˙Rμ=qmFHgλνFνρx˙ρ∂t∂xλx˙μt˙-Γνρλx˙νx˙ρ∂t∂xλx˙μt˙+x˙νx˙ρ∂t∂xν∂xρx˙μt˙.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Thus, we see that the use of the transformation formula greatly simplifies the task of readapting the Vlasov vector field from one kinematic domain to another. Furthermore, we will see below that Eq. (54) can be trivially derived from the Vlasov bivector. This is shown in Lemma 3.21.

The Vlasov bivectors

Here we present the Vlasov Bivector, the key goal of this article. The advantages of this approach have been listed in Sect. 1.2. The Vlasov bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ can always be expressed as70\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi = \mathcal {R} \wedge W, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧W,for any appropriate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU. It is trivial to see that any Vlasov fields related by Eqs. (51) and (54) produce the same Vlasov bivector, and so represents an entire class of equivalent Vlasov fields. Throughout this section, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ will be reserved for Vlasov bivectors while an arbitrary bivector will be denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ.

Bivectors

First, we write the definition of bivectors and explore some of their important properties.

Definition 3.1

(Bivector) A bivector over a manifold N is an exterior product of vector fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_\mu ,Y_\mu \in \Gamma \ TN$$\end{document}Xμ,Yμ∈ΓTN over N, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _\mu X_\mu \wedge Y_\mu$$\end{document}∑μXμ∧Yμ, for an arbitrary summation index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}μ. These follow the standard rules for exterior products, namely ‘f’-linearity and antisymmetry.

Definition 3.2

(Simple Bivectors) A bivector over N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ is said to be simple if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X,Y\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}X,Y∈ΓTN such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =X\wedge Y$$\end{document}Φ=X∧Y. The space of all simple bivectors (fields) over N is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma \mathcal {B}^2(N)$$\end{document}ΓB2(N).

We require that a Vlasov bivector be simple and take the form Eq. (70). In order to formally write the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi ,$$\end{document}Ψ, we first observe the following property of simple bivectors.

Definition 3.3

(Bivector Pairing) A bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _\mu X_\mu \wedge Y_\mu$$\end{document}∑μXμ∧Yμ acts on a pair of scalar fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F,G\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0U$$\end{document}F,G∈ΓΛ0U according to71\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _\mu \big (X_\mu \wedge Y_\mu )\langle F,G\rangle = \sum _\mu ( X_\mu \langle F\rangle Y_\mu \langle G\rangle - X_\mu \langle G\rangle Y_\mu \langle F\rangle \big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑μ(Xμ∧Yμ)⟨F,G⟩=∑μ(Xμ⟨F⟩Yμ⟨G⟩-Xμ⟨G⟩Yμ⟨F⟩).Similarly we may define a vector field using72\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _\mu (X_\mu \wedge Y_\mu )\langle F,\bullet \rangle = \sum _\mu \big ( X_\mu \langle F\rangle Y_\mu - \ Y_\mu \langle F\rangle X_\mu \big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑μ(Xμ∧Yμ)⟨F,∙⟩=∑μ(Xμ⟨F⟩Yμ-Yμ⟨F⟩Xμ).In the case that we have a simple bivector acting on a scalar field, we write73\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (X\wedge Y)\langle F,\bullet \rangle = X\langle F\rangle Y- Y\langle F\rangle X. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(X∧Y)⟨F,∙⟩=X⟨F⟩Y-Y⟨F⟩X.

Lemma 3.4

Given a bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ and a nonzero vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}X∈ΓTN, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\wedge \Phi =0$$\end{document}X∧Φ=0 if and only if there exists some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \Gamma \ TN$$\end{document}Y∈ΓTN such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =X\wedge Y$$\end{document}Φ=X∧Y.

Proof

Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\wedge \Phi =0$$\end{document}X∧Φ=0 and let N have dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell$$\end{document}ℓ. Define a local coordinate system such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=\partial _0$$\end{document}X=∂0 when we may write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =\Phi ^{0a}\partial _0\wedge \partial _a+\frac{1}{2} \Phi ^{bc}\partial _b \wedge \partial _c$$\end{document}Φ=Φ0a∂0∧∂a+12Φbc∂b∧∂c, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,b,c=1,\ldots ,\ell -1$$\end{document}a,b,c=1,…,ℓ-1. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _0\wedge \Phi = \frac{1}{2} \Phi ^{bc}\partial _0\wedge \partial _b\wedge \partial _c=0$$\end{document}∂0∧Φ=12Φbc∂0∧∂b∧∂c=0, we must have that each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{bc}=0$$\end{document}Φbc=0. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi = \partial _0\wedge (\Phi ^{0a}\partial _a)=X\wedge Y$$\end{document}Φ=∂0∧(Φ0a∂a)=X∧Y. The converse holds by the properties of the exterior product (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\wedge X=0$$\end{document}X∧X=0). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Definition 3.5

(Specially Related Pairs of Vector Fields) Two pairs of vector fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_1,X_2\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}X1,X2∈ΓTN and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_1,Y_2\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}Y1,Y2∈ΓTN are said to be specially related if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta ,\gamma ,\delta \in \Gamma \Lambda ^0N$$\end{document}α,β,γ,δ∈ΓΛ0N satisfying\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} ( \alpha \delta - \beta \gamma )\vert _p=1\; \forall p\in N, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(αδ-βγ)|p=1∀p∈N,such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} Y_1=\alpha X_1+\beta X_2, \text { and } Y_2=\gamma X_1+\delta X_2. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Y1=αX1+βX2,andY2=γX1+δX2.

Lemma 3.6

Two pairs of vector fields, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_1,X_2\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}X1,X2∈ΓTN and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_1,Y_2\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}Y1,Y2∈ΓTN, are specially related if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_1\wedge X_2= Y_1\wedge Y_2$$\end{document}X1∧X2=Y1∧Y2.

Proof

See Sect. A.2. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

For a Vlasov bivector to contain all the necessary information to perform plasma kinematics, it must satisfy three conditions: the radial condition, the horizontal condition, and a third condition. There are three equivalent ways to express the third condition: integrability, being radially cubic, and being expressible as in Eq. (70).

We begin by discussing integrability. A geometric interpretation of a bivector is a network of infinitesimal rectangles whose sides are defined by a pair of vectors. When these bivectors ‘knit together’ to form smooth surfaces, the vector distribution spanned by the components of the bivector is integrable. These surfaces are depicted in Fig. 5. More formally, we may consider integrability using the Frobenius theorem and the language of vector distributions (a method of smoothly assigning vector subspaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_xM$$\end{document}TxM to each point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in M$$\end{document}x∈M).

Definition 3.7

(Tangent Bivector) A bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2(N)$$\end{document}Φ∈ΓB2(N) is tangent to a surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K\subset N$$\end{document}K⊂N if, given a representation for the bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =X\wedge Y, \; X,Y\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}Φ=X∧Y,X,Y∈ΓTN, both X and Y are tangent to K in the sense described in Definition 1.2.

From Lemma 3.6, it is clear that Definition 3.7 is independent of the representation of the bivector: if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =X\wedge Y$$\end{document}Φ=X∧Y and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =Z\wedge V$$\end{document}Φ=Z∧V and X and Y are tangent to K, then by Lemma 3.6Z and V are linear combinations of X and Y and hence tangent to K.

Definition 3.8

(Integrability) A simple bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2(N)$$\end{document}Φ∈ΓB2(N) is said to be integrable if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X,Y\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}X,Y∈ΓTN and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta \in \Gamma \Lambda ^0N$$\end{document}α,β∈ΓΛ0N such that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =X\wedge Y$$\end{document}Φ=X∧Y then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[X,Y]=\alpha X+ \beta Y$$\end{document}[X,Y]=αX+βY. By Lemma 3.6, if one representation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ satisfies the integrability condition, then so do all other representations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ.

Lemma 3.9

If a bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2(N)$$\end{document}Φ∈ΓB2(N) is integrable then for any representation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =X\wedge Y,\; X,Y\in \Gamma TN$$\end{document}Φ=X∧Y,X,Y∈ΓTN, we have74\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}X,Y]=\alpha X+ \beta Y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[X,Y]=αX+βY,for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta \in \Gamma \Lambda ^0N$$\end{document}α,β∈ΓΛ0N.

Proof

See Sect. A.3. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

It follows from Lemmas 3.6 and 3.9 that integrability is a well-defined property. That is, given a simple bivector, if one representation satisfies the integrability condition, then all representations do.

The Frobenius theorem states that a vector distribution over a manifold M is integrable if and only if the Lie bracket of any two vectors within the distribution also lies within the distribution. An integrable vector distribution then admits a collection of maximal connected integral manifolds which form a foliation of M. Given an integrable bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =X\wedge Y$$\end{document}Φ=X∧Y, we can form a vector distribution over U which is spanned by X and Y. This generates a two-dimensional foliation of U such that X and Y are tangent to the leaves of our foliation at each point. We may identify the leaves of this foliation with the form manifolds of the particle density form.

We also introduce the null condition here. This is a property an arbitrary bivector may have which is necessary for defining the transport equations on U.

Definition 3.10

(Null condition) Given a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2n{-}2)$$\end{document}(2n-2)-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}α, and a bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2(N)$$\end{document}Φ∈ΓB2(N), then the null condition is given by75\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{gathered} \text{ Null }(\Phi ,\alpha ) \text { is true}\iff i_X\alpha =0\;\text {and}\; i_Y\alpha =0, \text{ for } \text{ any } X,Y\in \Gamma TN \text{ such } \text{ that } \Phi = X \wedge Y. \end{gathered} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Null(Φ,α)is true⟺iXα=0andiYα=0,foranyX,Y∈ΓTNsuchthatΦ=X∧Y.

To see that the null condition is well defined notice that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\wedge Y= Z\wedge V$$\end{document}X∧Y=Z∧V then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{ Null }(X\wedge Y,\alpha )$$\end{document}Null(X∧Y,α) holds if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{ Null } (Z\wedge V,\alpha )$$\end{document}Null(Z∧V,α) holds. This is due to Lemma 3.6 and the linearity of the contraction mapping.

Horizontal bivectors

The radial and the horizontal conditions are dealt with in tandem. We now exclusively work on the submanifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U\subset \breve{T}M$$\end{document}U⊂T˘M.

Definition 3.11

(Radial Bivectors) A simple bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2(U)$$\end{document}Φ∈ΓB2(U) is called radial if76\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {R}\wedge \Phi =0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R∧Φ=0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}R∈ΓTU is the radial vector field.

Definition 3.12

(Horizontal Bivectors) A simple radial bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2(U)$$\end{document}Φ∈ΓB2(U) is called horizontal if for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}f,h∈ΓΛ0M it satisfies77\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi \langle \pi ^*f, \dot{h}\rangle = -\dot{f}\dot{h}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ⟨π∗f,h˙⟩=-f˙h˙.The space of horizontal bivectors is denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma \mathcal {B}^2_H(U)$$\end{document}ΓBH2(U).

Note that in order for a bivector to be horizontal it must also be simple and radial. Hence, from this point onwards, when we refer to a horizontal bivector, we also assume it to be simple and radial.

Lemma 3.13

A bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =\mathcal {R}\wedge X$$\end{document}Φ=R∧X is horizontal if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}X∈ΓTU is horizontal.

Proof

Suppose first that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi = \mathcal {R}\wedge X$$\end{document}Φ=R∧X for some horizontal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}X∈ΓTU. We then have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\wedge \Psi =0$$\end{document}R∧Ψ=0 automatically; hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ is radial. To see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ is horizontal, observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Phi \langle \pi ^*f,\dot{h}\rangle =&\mathcal {R} \langle \pi ^*f \rangle X\langle \dot{h} \rangle - \mathcal {R} \langle \dot{h} \rangle X\langle \pi ^*f \rangle = -\dot{f}\dot{h}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ⟨π∗f,h˙⟩=R⟨π∗f⟩X⟨h˙⟩-R⟨h˙⟩X⟨π∗f⟩=-f˙h˙,for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}f,h∈ΓΛ0M.

Suppose now that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ is horizontal. By Lemma 3.4 (since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ is radial and simple), there exists some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}X∈ΓTU such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi = \mathcal {R}\wedge X. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ=R∧X.By the horizontal condition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} -\dot{f}\dot{h}= \Phi \langle \pi ^*f,\dot{h}\rangle =&\mathcal {R} \langle \pi ^*f \rangle X\langle \dot{h} \rangle - \mathcal {R} \langle \dot{h} \rangle X\langle \pi ^*f \rangle = -X\langle \pi ^*f \rangle \dot{h}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-f˙h˙=Φ⟨π∗f,h˙⟩=R⟨π∗f⟩X⟨h˙⟩-R⟨h˙⟩X⟨π∗f⟩=-X⟨π∗f⟩h˙,for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}f,h∈ΓΛ0M. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X \langle \pi ^*f \rangle =\dot{f}$$\end{document}X⟨π∗f⟩=f˙, that is, X is horizontal. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Lemma 3.14

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}X∈ΓTU and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2(U)$$\end{document}Φ∈ΓB2(U) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =\mathcal {R}\wedge X$$\end{document}Φ=R∧X. If X is horizontal and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ is integrable, then there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \Gamma \Lambda ^0U$$\end{document}α∈ΓΛ0U such that78\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {R},X]=X+\alpha \mathcal {R}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[R,X]=X+αR.

Proof

Since X is horizontal, observe that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}f∈ΓΛ0M we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {R},X]\langle \pi ^*f\rangle =&\mathcal {R}\langle X\langle \pi ^*f\rangle \rangle - X\langle \mathcal {R}\langle \pi ^*f \rangle \rangle = \mathcal {R}\langle \dot{f}\rangle =\dot{f}=X\langle \pi ^*f\rangle . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[R,X]⟨π∗f⟩=R⟨X⟨π∗f⟩⟩-X⟨R⟨π∗f⟩⟩=R⟨f˙⟩=f˙=X⟨π∗f⟩.By the integrability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ,$$\end{document}Φ, we also have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {R},X]=\alpha \mathcal {R}+ \beta X$$\end{document}[R,X]=αR+βX so that we also have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {R},X]\langle \pi ^*f\rangle = \alpha \mathcal {R}\langle \pi ^*f\rangle +\beta X\langle \pi ^*f \rangle = \beta X\langle \pi ^*f \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[R,X]⟨π∗f⟩=αR⟨π∗f⟩+βX⟨π∗f⟩=βX⟨π∗f⟩.Combining the last two equations gives us that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =1$$\end{document}β=1. Hence, the result holds. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Vlasov bivectors

With this we have all the necessary ingredients to define a Vlasov bivector. There are several equivalent properties which ensure that a bivector contains sufficient structure. Given a horizontal bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2_H(U)$$\end{document}Ψ∈ΓBH2(U), these three properties are, the existence of a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧W, integrability, and another property we call the radially cubic property. Representations and integrability are discussed in Sect. 3.1, and the radially cubic property is discussed below.

Definition 3.15

(Radially Cubic) A horizontal bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2_H(U)$$\end{document}Ψ∈ΓBH2(U) is radially cubic if for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0 M$$\end{document}f,h∈ΓΛ0M, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0 we have79\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi \langle \dot{f},\dot{h} \rangle \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}= \lambda ^3 \Psi \langle \dot{f},\dot{h} \rangle \vert _{\underline{u}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ψ⟨f˙,h˙⟩|λu_=λ3Ψ⟨f˙,h˙⟩|u_.

We see below in Theorem 3.18 that we can generate a Vlasov field W, using a Vlasov bivector and a kinematic indicator. A natural choice to use is a ‘lab-time’ kinematic indicator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{t}$$\end{document}t˙. However, in general there is no \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}t∈ΓΛ0M such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{t}\ne 0$$\end{document}t˙≠0 on all of U. As an intermediate step, we can always define the following kinematic indicator using the coordinate system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^0,\ldots x^{n-1})$$\end{document}(x0,…xn-1) on M,80\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_{crd }= \sum _{\mu =0}^{n-1} (\dot{x}^\mu )^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fcrd=∑μ=0n-1(x˙μ)2.It is clear this is not a physical kinematic indicator since it depends on the coordinate system. It is homogeneous degree 2. Since this is even, one would need the causality indicator to define a kinematic domain, Eq. (36).

Definition 3.16

(Coordinate-based Vlasov field) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^0,\ldots x^{n-1})$$\end{document}(x0,…xn-1) be a local coordinate system on M and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}_H^2(U)$$\end{document}Ψ∈ΓBH2(U), we define the coordinate-based Vlasov field by81\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W_{crd }= \frac{\Psi \langle F_{crd },\bullet \rangle }{2F_{crd }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Wcrd=Ψ⟨Fcrd,∙⟩2Fcrd.

Lemma 3.17

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2_H(U)$$\end{document}Ψ∈ΓBH2(U) be radially cubic and then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{crd }$$\end{document}Wcrd as given by Eq. (81) is a Vlasov field such that82\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W_{crd }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧Wcrd.Furthermore, given another representation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ, say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge X$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧X for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}X∈ΓTU, then83\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W_{crd }=X- \frac{X\langle F_{crd }\rangle }{2F_{crd }}\mathcal {R}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Wcrd=X-X⟨Fcrd⟩2FcrdR.

Proof

See Sect. A.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Theorem 3.18

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2_H(U)$$\end{document}Ψ∈ΓBH2(U) be a horizontal bivector. The following properties are equivalent: (i)There exists a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧W.(ii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is integrable (Definition 3.8).(iii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is radially cubic (Definition 3.15).

(i)There exists a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧W.

There exists a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧W.

(ii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is integrable (Definition 3.8).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is integrable (Definition 3.8).

(iii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is radially cubic (Definition 3.15).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is radially cubic (Definition 3.15).

Proof

Suppose first that there exists a Vlasov field W such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi = \mathcal {R}\wedge W$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧W. To see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is radially cubic observe that, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,h\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}f,h∈ΓΛ0M, any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}λ≠0, it follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Psi \langle \dot{f},\dot{h} \rangle \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}&= \mathcal {R} \langle \dot{f} \rangle W\langle \dot{h} \rangle \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}}- \mathcal {R} \langle \dot{h} \rangle W\langle \dot{f} \rangle \vert _{\lambda \underline{u}} = \lambda \mathcal {R} \langle \dot{f} \rangle \lambda ^2 W\langle \dot{h} \rangle \vert _{\underline{u}} -\lambda \mathcal {R} \langle \dot{h} \rangle \lambda ^2 W_i\langle \dot{f} \rangle \vert _{\underline{u}} = \lambda ^3 \Psi \langle \dot{f},\dot{h} \rangle \vert _{\underline{u}}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ψ⟨f˙,h˙⟩|λu_=R⟨f˙⟩W⟨h˙⟩|λu_-R⟨h˙⟩W⟨f˙⟩|λu_=λR⟨f˙⟩λ2W⟨h˙⟩|u_-λR⟨h˙⟩λ2Wi⟨f˙⟩|u_=λ3Ψ⟨f˙,h˙⟩|u_.Also, since W is radially quadratic we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {R},W]=W$$\end{document}[R,W]=W by Lemma 2.4. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is integrable. That is, property (i) implies properties (ii) and (iii).

By Lemma 3.17, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is radially cubic then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{crd }$$\end{document}Wcrd as defined by Eq. (81) is a Vlasov field and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W_{crd }$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧Wcrd. Hence, property (iii) implies (i).

Suppose now that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is integrable. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{crd }$$\end{document}Wcrd be given by Eq. (81). By Lemma 3.17, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W_{crd }$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧Wcrd and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{crd }$$\end{document}Wcrd is horizontal. It remains to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{crd }$$\end{document}Wcrd is radially quadratic. By Lemma 3.13, there exists a horizontal vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}X∈ΓTU such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge X$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧X and by Lemma 3.17, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{crd }$$\end{document}Wcrd and X are related by Eq. (83). The integrability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ tells us \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {R},X]=\alpha \mathcal {R}+ \beta X$$\end{document}[R,X]=αR+βX for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta \in \Gamma \Lambda ^0 U$$\end{document}α,β∈ΓΛ0U. It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =1$$\end{document}β=1 form Lemma 3.14. We then have that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} [\mathcal {R},W_{crd }]&= [\mathcal {R},X]- \left[ \mathcal {R},\frac{X\langle \dot{f}\rangle }{\dot{f}} \mathcal {R} \right] = [ \mathcal {R},X] -\mathcal {R} \bigg \langle \frac{X\langle \dot{f}\rangle }{\dot{f}} \bigg \rangle \mathcal {R} = X+ \alpha \mathcal {R} -\left( \frac{\mathcal {R}\langle X\langle \dot{f}\rangle \rangle }{\dot{f}}- \frac{X\langle \dot{f}\rangle }{\dot{f}} \right) \mathcal {R} \\{}&= X+\alpha \mathcal {R} -\frac{1}{\dot{f}} [\mathcal {R},X]\langle \dot{f}\rangle \mathcal {R} = X+\alpha \mathcal {R} -\frac{1}{\dot{f}} (X\langle \dot{f}\rangle +\alpha \dot{f})\mathcal {R} = X-\frac{X\langle \dot{f}\rangle }{\dot{f}}\mathcal {R} = W_{crd }. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[R,Wcrd]=[R,X]-R,X⟨f˙⟩f˙R=[R,X]-R〈X⟨f˙⟩f˙〉R=X+αR-R⟨X⟨f˙⟩⟩f˙-X⟨f˙⟩f˙R=X+αR-1f˙[R,X]⟨f˙⟩R=X+αR-1f˙(X⟨f˙⟩+αf˙)R=X-X⟨f˙⟩f˙R=Wcrd.Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{crd }$$\end{document}Wcrd is radially quadratic by Lemma 2.4. It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{crd }$$\end{document}Wcrd is a Vlasov field and property (ii) implies property (i). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Definition 3.19

(Vlasov Bivectors) A horizontal bivector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2_H(U)$$\end{document}Ψ∈ΓBH2(U) defines a Vlasov bivector if it satisfies one of the following equivalent properties: There exists a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧W.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is integrable (Definition 3.8).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is radially cubic (Definition 3.15).The space of Vlasov vectors over U is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}^2_V(U)$$\end{document}Ψ∈ΓBV2(U).

There exists a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧W.

There exists a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma TU$$\end{document}W∈ΓTU such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧W.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is integrable (Definition 3.8).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is integrable (Definition 3.8).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is radially cubic (Definition 3.15).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document}Ψ is radially cubic (Definition 3.15).

Lemma 3.20

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \Gamma \mathcal {B}_V^2(U)$$\end{document}Ψ∈ΓBV2(U), let E have a kinematic indicator F of order k, and let84\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {W}_F=\frac{\Psi \left\langle {F},\bullet \right\rangle }{k F}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}WF=ΨF,∙kF,Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_F$$\end{document}WF is a Vlasov field which is compatible with F and85\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi = \mathcal {R}\wedge W_F. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧WF.

Proof

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi =\mathcal {R}\wedge W_F$$\end{document}Ψ=R∧WF and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_F$$\end{document}WF is horizontal by the same logic as Lemma 3.17.
