Lemma 4.13

The current \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n{-}1)$$\end{document}(n-1)-form on U, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}=\pi _\varsigma (\chi \wedge \theta )$$\end{document}J=πς(χ∧θ), satisfies the continuity equation98\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {d}\mathcal {J}=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dJ=0.

Proof

The exterior derivative commutes with the de Rham pushforward and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\theta =0$$\end{document}dθ=0 so we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {d}\mathcal {J}= \text {d}\pi _\varsigma (\chi \wedge \theta )= \pi _\varsigma (\text {d}\chi \wedge \theta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dJ=dπς(χ∧θ)=πς(dχ∧θ).Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J is independent of our choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ by Lemma 4.12 it suffices to pick \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\chi =0$$\end{document}dχ=0. By picking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0 U$$\end{document}r∈ΓΛ0U such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$dr\ne 0$$\end{document}dr≠0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\langle r \rangle =1$$\end{document}R⟨r⟩=1, we may define a coordinate system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^\mu ,r,y^{a})$$\end{document}(xμ,r,ya). By choosing\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi =\chi _r(r)\text {d}r, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ=χr(r)dr,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _r(r)$$\end{document}χr(r) is a function in r with compact support, to comply with Eq. (94), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\chi =0$$\end{document}dχ=0. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\mathcal {J}=0$$\end{document}dJ=0, and charge is conserved. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Lemma 4.14

Let U be time-orientable, E be a kinematic domain and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ is future time pointing. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _E$$\end{document}θE be related by Eqs. (89) and (91). The current form on U, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}\in \Gamma \Lambda ^{n-1}M$$\end{document}J∈ΓΛn-1M (Definition 4.10) and the current form on E, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}_E\in \Gamma \Lambda ^{n-1}M$$\end{document}JE∈ΓΛn-1M, Definition 4.8, are identical.

Proof

Let F be the 1-homogeneous kinematic indicator associated with E, define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} r=\log F, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}r=logF,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{r_0}=\{ \underline{u}\in U :r\vert _{\underline{u}}=r_0 \}$$\end{document}Kr0={u_∈U:r|u_=r0}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{r_0}:K_{r_0}\hookrightarrow U$$\end{document}Σr0:Kr0↪U. First observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=K_0$$\end{document}E=K0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\langle r\rangle =1$$\end{document}R⟨r⟩=1. By application of Lemma 4.11, we have for any test form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi \in \Gamma _0\Lambda ^1M$$\end{document}ϕ∈Γ0Λ1M,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \int \limits _U \pi ^*\phi \wedge \theta \wedge \chi&= \int \limits _{p\in K_0} \Sigma _0^*(\pi ^*\phi \wedge \theta ) \left( \int \limits _{\mathbb {R}} \eta _p^*\chi \right) = \int \limits _{ K_0} \Sigma _0^*(\pi ^*\phi \wedge \theta ) = \int \limits _{ E} \Sigma _E^*(\pi ^*\phi \wedge \theta ) = \int \limits _E \pi _E^*\phi \wedge \theta _E. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Uπ∗ϕ∧θ∧χ=∫p∈K0Σ0∗(π∗ϕ∧θ)∫Rηp∗χ=∫K0Σ0∗(π∗ϕ∧θ)=∫EΣE∗(π∗ϕ∧θ)=∫EπE∗ϕ∧θE.Hence, for any E, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}=\mathcal {J}_E$$\end{document}J=JE. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hfill\square$$\end{document}□

Using the coordinate expression for the fibre integral in [33], the coefficients of the current vector on the unit hyperboloid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH (i.e. the 4 current when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dim M =4$$\end{document}dimM=4) are given by99\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {J}_{E_{\text {H}}}^{\mu }=\int \limits _{E_{\text {H}}}\dot{x}^\mu f_{E_{\text {H}}} \frac{\sqrt{-\det g}}{\dot{x}_0} {\text {d}}\dot{x}^{1,\ldots ,n-1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}JEHμ=∫EHx˙μfEH-detgx˙0dx˙1,…,n-1,where100\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {J}_{E_{\text {H}}}^\mu = \star ({\text {d}}x^\mu \wedge \mathcal {J}_{E_{\text {H}}}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}JEHμ=⋆(dxμ∧JEH).

Discussion about the stress–energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n{-}1$$\end{document}n-1-form

Given a kinematic domain E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \Gamma \Lambda ^1M$$\end{document}α∈ΓΛ1M, the stress–energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n{-}1)$$\end{document}(n-1)-form can be expressed101\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau ^E_{\alpha }= \pi _E{}_\varsigma (\hat{\alpha } \theta _E), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ταE=πEς(α^θE),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\alpha }\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0U$$\end{document}α^∈ΓΛ0U is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\alpha }\vert _{\underline{u}}=\alpha :\underline{u}$$\end{document}α^|u_=α:u_ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}\in U$$\end{document}u_∈U. The stress–energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n{-}1$$\end{document}n-1-form for the unit hyperboloid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH can be converted into the usual stress–energy tensor102\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_{E_{\text {H}}}^{\mu \nu }=\int \limits _{E_{\text {H}}}\dot{x}^\mu \dot{x}^\nu f_{E_{\text {H}}} \frac{\sqrt{-\det g}}{\dot{x}_0} {\text {d}}\dot{x}^{1,\ldots ,n-1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TEHμν=∫EHx˙μx˙νfEH-detgx˙0dx˙1,…,n-1,using the relationship103\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_{E_{\text {H}}}^{\mu \nu } = \star ({\text {d}}x^\mu \wedge \tau ^{E_{\text {H}}}_{{\text {d}}x^\nu }), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TEHμν=⋆(dxμ∧τdxνH),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\star$$\end{document}⋆ is the Hodge dual. See Lemma A.8 for proof of this statement. This can be substituted into the right-hand side of Einstein’s equations to complete the Einstein–Vlasov system on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH.

Due to the similarities between the definitions of the current \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n{-}1)$$\end{document}(n-1)-form, Eq. (93), and the stress–energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n{-}1)$$\end{document}(n-1)-form, Eq. (101)), it may be tempting to try defining the stress–energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n{-}1)$$\end{document}(n-1)-form on U by104\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau = \pi _\varsigma (\chi \wedge \hat{\alpha }\theta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τ=πς(χ∧α^θ).Unfortunately, unlike for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J, the stress–energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n{-}1)$$\end{document}(n-1)-form depends on the choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ. This is because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\left( \hat{\alpha } \theta \right) \ne 0$$\end{document}dα^θ≠0 in general, so that Lemma 4.14 does not apply.

For an explicit example of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ^E_{\alpha }$$\end{document}ταE depending on E, consider the charged free dust in Minkowski space time so that in Cartesian coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W=\dot{x}^\mu \partial ^{(x)}_\mu$$\end{document}W=x˙μ∂μ(x). In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ^{E_{\text {H}}}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}ταH and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ^{E_{t}}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}ταt are given by105\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau ^{E_{\text {H}}}_{\alpha }&= i^{(x)}_\mu {\text {d}}x^{0,\cdots ,n-1} \int \limits _{E_{\text {H}}} f_{E_{\text {H}}} \alpha _{a} \frac{\dot{x}^{a}}{\dot{x}_0} \dot{x}^\mu {\text {d}}\dot{x}^{1,\cdots ,n-1},\end{aligned}$$\end{document}ταH=iμ(x)dx0,⋯,n-1∫EHfEHαax˙ax˙0x˙μdx˙1,⋯,n-1,106\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau ^{E_{t}}_{\alpha }&= i^{(x)}_\mu {\text {d}}x^{0,\cdots ,n-1} \int \limits _{E_{t}} f_{E_{t}} \alpha _{a} \dot{x}^{a} \dot{x}^\mu {\text {d}}\dot{x}^{1,\cdots ,n-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ταt=iμ(x)dx0,⋯,n-1∫EtfEtαax˙ax˙μdx˙1,⋯,n-1.in their respective adapted coordinate systems.

Furthermore, in general, the stress–energy form is not necessarily divergenceless (in the sense put forth in [34]) for an arbitrary lab frame with no electromagnetic interaction. Using the external covariant derivative D,107\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D \tau ^E_{\alpha }= {\text {d}} \tau ^E_{\alpha } - {\text {d}}x^\mu \wedge \tau ^E_{\nabla _\mu \alpha }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DταE=dταE-dxμ∧τ∇μαE,the divergenceless condition can then be written \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \tau ^E_{\alpha }=0$$\end{document}DταE=0. In [35] it’s shown that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \tau ^{E_{\text {H}}}_{\alpha }=0$$\end{document}DταH=0. However, for an arbitrary lab bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{t}$$\end{document}Et, it can be shown that108\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D \tau ^{E_{t}}_{{\text {d}}x^{\underline{\mu }}}=0, \quad D\tau ^{E_{t}}_{{\text {d}}x^0}= {\text {d}}x^{0,\cdots ,n-1} \int _{\pi ^{-1}(\bullet )} \Gamma ^0_{\mu \nu } \dot{x}^\nu \dot{x}^\mu {\text {d}}\dot{x}^{1,\cdots ,n-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dτdxμ_t=0,Dτdx0t=dx0,⋯,n-1∫π-1(∙)Γμν0x˙νx˙μdx˙1,⋯,n-1.in an adapted coordinate system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^\mu ,\dot{x}^{a})$$\end{document}(xμ,x˙a).

The usual stress–energy form is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ^{E_{\text {H}}}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}ταH, which is needed for the Einstein–Vlasov system, is divergenceless. This implies there is a preferred kinematic domain, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH. One may ask whether there are still advantages for using the formalism in this article. We aregue that there are for two reasons. First, it makes it clear how the Vlasov equations, i.e. the Vlasov bivector and the transport equations, depend on the metric. This may be less explicit in the usual treatment. This is especially relevant if one needs to vary the metric, as is done in [36], for instance. Second, it reveals the relationship between the stress–energy tensor and the kinematic domain. Since we do not have a formulation of the stress–energy form/tensor which does not depend on the kinematic domain, we cannot assert that Einstein–Vlasov systems are parameter-free under our formalism.

Another use of our approach, is when considering non-metric compatible connections. As stated in the introduction, the trajectories no longer remain on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH, see Fig. 2. However, using the Vlasov bivector and the particle density form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ, this is no longer a problem. After calculating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ, one can choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}EH to calculate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ^{E_{\text {H}}}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}ταH.

It is an open question to see whether there is a relationship between the stress–energy forms for different E’s or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ’s or whether there exists an object similar to the stress–energy form can be defined which does not depend on the choice of E or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ.

Application to numerical simulation of plasmas

This work can be applied to numerical simulations of plasmas. Such simulations typically involve solving the Maxwell–Vlasov system, either directly (particularly in one or two dimensions), or through PIC methods.

The usual method for describing the relativistic Maxwell–Vlasov system is in terms of proper time. However, for numerical simulations it is much easier to use the lab time. This is explained in Sect. 5.1.

A particular numerical simulation procedure is the agile numerical integrator (ANI). To date, this method is mainly used where velocities are non-relativistic. If velocities start approaching the speed of light, simulation becomes increasingly challenging as it would require many grid points near \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=c$$\end{document}v=c. We show how one can extend the applicability of the ANI, using a series of Lorentz transformations, to scenarios where the velocities of particles become large, but the relative velocities between the particles remains small. This is ideal for cases of particle acceleration, as is detailed in Sect. 5.2.

The use of lab time

Fig. 7Advantages of using a lab frame when doing numerical simulations. The red planes represent time slices. We integrate over v to evaluate the charge and current. These are the fibres depicted in dark red. By contrast, using proper time to step forward from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _0=t_0$$\end{document}τ0=t0 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _0+\Delta \tau _0$$\end{document}τ0+Δτ0 results in the blue surface which cannot be directly integrated

Advantages of using a lab frame when doing numerical simulations. The red planes represent time slices. We integrate over v to evaluate the charge and current. These are the fibres depicted in dark red. By contrast, using proper time to step forward from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _0=t_0$$\end{document}τ0=t0 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _0+\Delta \tau _0$$\end{document}τ0+Δτ0 results in the blue surface which cannot be directly integrated

These simulations invariably use a lab frame. Recall that the term lab frame in this article refers to any kinematic domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_t$$\end{document}Et, which is determined by a time-slicing scalar field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in \Gamma \Lambda ^0M$$\end{document}t∈ΓΛ0M. This may be the frame of an actual lab, but can include the rest frame of a particle.

It is challenging to use the proper-time Lorentz force equations to update the particle trajectory. This is because, assuming \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x^0(\tau )$$\end{document}x0(τ) all coincided, the updated \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x^0(\tau +\Delta \tau )$$\end{document}x0(τ+Δτ) would not coincide. This would make it difficult to sum over the particles in the same cell to obtain the charge or current. The same problem arises when attempting to solve the Maxwell–Vlasov system with the proper-time version of Vlasov’s equation, since the integration over velocities cannot be performed directly. This is depicted in Fig. 7.

Assuming a numerical simulation scheme is using a lab frame \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_t$$\end{document}Et, one needs to choose time-slicing scalar field t. This decision is made alongside many others, such as coordinates, grid strategy, integrator, etc. The usual choice for Minkowski spacetime is an inertial frame. However, there may be advantages with using a non-inertial frame. For example, in a particle accelerator, one could use the frame adapted to the design orbit. For uniform acceleration, this would correspond to Rindler coordinates.

In general relativity, there are generally no preferred coordinate systems, and the choice of time-slicing may be arbitrary. In this case, it may be useful to be able to run the simulations in different coordinate systems and compare the result.

Let t be one time-slicing and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{t}=\hat{t}(t,x^a)$$\end{document}t^=t^(t,xa) be a new time-slicing. Then from Eq. (12)109\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\hat{t}} = \frac{\partial \hat{t}}{\partial t} \dot{t} + \frac{\partial \hat{t}}{\partial x^a} \dot{x}^a, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}t^˙=∂t^∂tt˙+∂t^∂xax˙a,since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\hat{t}}$$\end{document}t^˙ is a kinematic indicator it can be directly placed into Eq. (54) to give the transformed Vlasov field.

The agile numerical integrator

Our formalism enables a generalisation of work such as [11] wherein a novel integration scheme, the agile numerical (ANI), is derived from the treatment of the Vlasov field as the divergence of a flux density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J over phase space. As stated in the introduction, the ANI is mainly used to model particles with non-relativistic velocities. Here we show how to extend, using a Lorentz transformation, the ANI to higher velocities, giving it application for particle accelerators, or even the electrons inside the bubble of a wakefield accelerator.

In the aforementioned paper, the linearised Vlasov–Poisson system is described by the flux density110\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {J} = f \hat{t} + v_x f\hat{x} - \frac{q}{m} \frac{\partial \phi }{\partial x}f \hat{v}_x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J=ft^+vxfx^-qm∂ϕ∂xfv^x,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{t}$$\end{document}t^ (resp. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{x},\;\hat{v}_x$$\end{document}x^,v^x) are unit vectors in the t (resp. x, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_x$$\end{document}vx) direction, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi$$\end{document}ϕ is the electric potential satisfying the Poisson equation. This enables the Vlasov equation to be cast as111\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla \cdot \mathcal {J} =0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇·J=0,or an equivalent flux integral.

We can identify the coefficients of the phase space flux density Eq. (110) with the coefficients of the particle density form Definition 2.19. In particular, assuming the Vlasov–Poisson system to be written on a lab-time bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{t}$$\end{document}Et, we may make the following identifications: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_x=\dot{x}$$\end{document}vx=x˙, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{t}=\partial _t$$\end{document}t^=∂t, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{x}=\partial _x$$\end{document}x^=∂x, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{v}_x=\partial _{\dot{x}},$$\end{document}v^x=∂x˙, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{E_{t}}={\text {d}}t\wedge {\text {d}}x\wedge d\dot{x}$$\end{document}ΩEt=dt∧dx∧dx˙ which satisfies Eq. (56). This allows us to recognise the notion of a velocity field on a phase space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{u}$$\end{document}u_ from [11] with our notion of a Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{E_{t}}$$\end{document}WEt on a kinematic domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{t}$$\end{document}Et. From here we can make the identification,112\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {J}=f_{E_{t}}W_{E_{t}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J=fEtWEt.Consequently Eq. (111) is equivalent to the transport equations Eq. (58), and the Vlasov equation can be written as113\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_{W_{E_{t}}}\left( f_{E_{t}} \Omega _{E_{t}} \right) = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LWEtfEtΩEt=0.Consequently, our formalism allows for the generalisation of the ANI. We illustrate this through an application by performing a Lorentz boost to the Vlasov–Poisson system.

The main benefit of the ANI is that by characterising the Vlasov equation as a vector field, the particle density function (PDF) can be evolved independently along each dynamical axis. This allows us to write the equation for the evolution of the PDF with fewer interpolations than a method invoking Strang splitting. For the relativistic Vlasov–Poisson system, the discretised Vlasov equation yields114\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(t+\Delta t,x,\dot{x})= f(t,x-\dot{x}\Delta t, \dot{x}) +f\left( t,x,\dot{x}-\frac{q}{m}\mathcal {E} F_{\text {H}}^{3/2} \Delta t \right) -f(t,x,\dot{x}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(t+Δt,x,x˙)=f(t,x-x˙Δt,x˙)+ft,x,x˙-qmEFH3/2Δt-f(t,x,x˙),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{\text {H}}$$\end{document}FH is given by Eq. (20), which is equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{\text {H}}=1-\dot{x}^2$$\end{document}FH=1-x˙2 here, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}$$\end{document}E is the electric field.

Note that this scheme requires 2 interpolations, one in x and one in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{x}$$\end{document}x˙. By gridding phase space in intervals width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Delta t, \Delta x, \Delta \dot{x})$$\end{document}(Δt,Δx,Δx˙) and indexing the nodes on the grid such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^n_{j,k}= f(n\Delta t, j\Delta x, k\Delta \dot{x})$$\end{document}fj,kn=f(nΔt,jΔx,kΔx˙), the above equation can be written115\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f^{n+1}_{j,k}= \sum _{j'} \mu ^0_{j,j'}(x-\dot{x}\Delta t)\ f^n_{j',k} + \sum _{k'} \mu ^1_{k,k'}\left( \dot{x}-\frac{q}{m}\mathcal {E} F_{\text {H}}^{3/2} \Delta t \right) \ f^n_{j,k'} -f^n_{j,k} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fj,kn+1=∑j′μj,j′0(x-x˙Δt)fj′,kn+∑k′μk,k′1x˙-qmEFH3/2Δtfj,k′n-fj,knwhere the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^0_{j,j'}(x)$$\end{document}μj,j′0(x) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^1_{k,k'}(\dot{x})$$\end{document}μk,k′1(x˙) are the interpolation parameters, e.g. [37].

The number of interpolations can be halved by defining the grid points as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=j\Delta x$$\end{document}x=jΔx and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{x}=k \Delta {x}/\Delta t$$\end{document}x˙=kΔx/Δt. Substituting into (114) gives116\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f^{n+1}_{j,k}= f^n_{j-k,k} + \sum _{k'} \mu ^1_{k,k'}\left( \dot{x}-\frac{q}{m}\mathcal {E} F_{\text {H}}^{3/2} \Delta t \right) \ f^n_{j,k'} -f^n_{j,k}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fj,kn+1=fj-k,kn+∑k′μk,k′1x˙-qmEFH3/2Δtfj,k′n-fj,kn.We can still obey the Courant stability conditions, by choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta t$$\end{document}Δt such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta t \le \Delta x /V_0$$\end{document}Δt≤Δx/V0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta t \le (\Delta x/(\frac{q}{m} {\mathcal {E}_0} F_H^{3/2}))^{1/2}$$\end{document}Δt≤(Δx/(qmE0FH3/2))1/2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_0$$\end{document}V0 is the maximum velocity of the system, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_0$$\end{document}E0 is the maximum electric field amplitude. When the above grid spacing is chosen, we call this the matched grid case.

As stated in the introduction, if the particles become ultra-relativistic so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{x}\rightarrow c$$\end{document}x˙→c, the use of the ANI becomes challenging as, to resolve the velocities, we would need many velocity grid points near \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{x}=c$$\end{document}x˙=c. Using Eq. (115), this is possible, but not ideal, whereas using Eq. (116) this is not possible as the grid size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \dot{x}$$\end{document}Δx˙ is fixed. Assuming the energy spread of the particles remains small, the solution is to perform a Lorentz boost, and continue the simulation in a grid adapted to this new rest frame. We show here how our technology can be used to transform the Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{E_{t}}$$\end{document}WEt and the PDF \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{E_{t}}$$\end{document}fEt to the new frame.

Suppose we have a Vlasov–Poisson system on the lab bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{t}$$\end{document}Et with PDF \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{E_{t}}$$\end{document}fEt, and corresponding Vlasov field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{E_{t}}$$\end{document}WEt. To find the equivalent of the PDF evolution equation [Eq. (114)] in the boosted frame \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{t'}$$\end{document}Et′, we first need to deduce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{E_{t'}}$$\end{document}WEt′ using Theorem 2.18. Next, we find an appropriate choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega$$\end{document}Ω to calculate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{E_{t}}$$\end{document}θEt using Eq. (57) and use this to deduce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{E_{t'}}$$\end{document}θEt′ by promoting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{E_{t}}$$\end{document}θEt to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^+$$\end{document}θ+ using Lemma (4.5). Then we use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^+$$\end{document}θ+ to calculate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{E_{t'}}$$\end{document}θEt′ through Lemma (4.6), which gives us a relationship between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{E_{t}}$$\end{document}fEt and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{E_{t'}}$$\end{document}fEt′ using Eq. (57).

Using Eq. (34), we can write the 1D1V Vlasov–Poisson field on U (the light cone), in a coordinate system adapted to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{t}$$\end{document}Et, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x,\dot{t},\dot{x})$$\end{document}(t,x,t˙,x˙), using the Lorentz metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_{\mu \nu }=\text{ diag }(-1,1)$$\end{document}gμν=diag(-1,1) as117\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W=\dot{t}\partial _t +\dot{x}\partial _x -\frac{q}{m}\mathcal {E}\frac{F_H^{3/2}}{\dot{t}} \partial _{\dot{x}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W=t˙∂t+x˙∂x-qmEFH3/2t˙∂x˙,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}=\mathcal {E}(t,x)$$\end{document}E=E(t,x) is the electric field, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_H$$\end{document}FH is Eq. Conclusion

In this article, we have presented an alternative way of representing the Vlasov equation. The primary advantage of this representation is that it enables the user to write the equations of motion without reference to a kinematic domain. As a result, the transformation laws of the Vlasov field become apparent and transformations between frames can be easily calculated in our framework.

The generality of our formalism makes it applicable to a wide variety of situations where the usual tools of kinematics would otherwise fail. For example, these tools apply in situations where there cannot exist a time orientation, when one does not have a metric compatible connection, or when working in a pre-metric context. Additionally, one can investigate the Vlasov equation for light-like particles. This enables the extension of the ultra-relativistic approximation [6] from fluids to the Vlasov equation. This is particularly relevant when considering particles in particle accelerators as well as astrophysical plasmas near black holes and neutron stars.

The current 3-form and the transport equations are generalised to our formalism. However, the generalisation of stress–energy 3-form depends on the choice of kinematic domain or a support form. Furthermore, the divergence of the stress–energy 3-form also depends on the choice of kinematic domain. As a result, it may be divergence-free with respect to one E, but not another. Since the stress–energy tensor is required for Einstein–Vlasov systems, it will be important, in future work, to investigate the relationship between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ^E_{\alpha }$$\end{document}ταE and its divergence for different kinematic domains.

As highlighted in Sect. 5, our work has implications for numerical simulations, in particular when these simulations concern coordinate transformations, non-inertial frames or general relativity. The formulation of the (relativistic) Vlasov equation [38] in terms of the particle density form, i.e. the transport equations, allows for the generalisation of some existing numerical integrators; for example, the agile numerical integrator [11]. Our formalism enables the extension of the ANI to include ultra-relativistic scenarios, that is, where the velocities approach the speed of light. For example, when electrons are accelerated up to ultra-relativistic velocities, but the spread in their energies remains small. In Sect. 5.2, we show that this is achieved by performing a series of Lorentz boosts, so that the PDF is always in a frame where velocities are not close to the speed of light. These techniques may be generalised to other numerical integrators which see applications in accelerator physics. For future work, one can look at applying this technique to 2d Vlasov solvers. This will enable the modelling of the transfer for energy from high energy electrons to electromagnetic waves using a wiggler or undulator, generalising work such as [39].

There are many interesting plasma phenomena like Landau damping, two stream instability, and plasma echos which can be further investigated with these tools. The basic theory of these starts with a (relativistic) Poisson–Vlasov system, for example [38]. As such, they have a preferred inertial lab frame and corresponding time-slicing. The power of the results in this article will become apparent if one wishes to reformulated the Poisson–Vlasov systems in a non-inertial lab frame or with respect to a more complicated kinematic domain. For example, a scenario where there is a magnetic field in the inertial lab frame, but no magnetic field in an adapted co-moving accelerating lab frame.

There is clearly a deep relationship between the work presented here and the idea of trajectory and solutions of second-order ODEs which do not have a parameter prescribed. In a follow-up article, we will show how to define such trajectories and their connection to the leaves of the Vlasov bivector.

Other directions one may consider are to look at the relationship of this work with jet bundles and Finsler geometry. One can also look at how to generalise the Vlasov equation and the Boltzmann equation. In this latter case, we may have to replace the transport equation Sect. 86, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Null}(\Psi ,\theta )$$\end{document}Null(Ψ,θ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\pi _\varsigma \theta =0$$\end{document}dπςθ=0.

In summary, we argue that the Vlasov bivector is the fundamental object to describe kinetic systems, since it is invariant under reparameterisation.
